[trialAccount t18280221-167]
647. RICHARD MARCH was indicted for that he, on the 12th of September, unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously, did accuse George Daniel Wilson of a crime punishble by law with transportation, (i. e.) with feloniously stealing from the person of the said Richard March a 10l. Bank note, his property, with intent to extort from the said George Daniel Wilson the sum of 10l., his monies; against the Statute.
MR. ALLEY conducted the prosecution.
PETER HARDY, ESQ. I am a Magistrate of the County of Middlesex. The prisoner came to me on the evening of the 12th of September, to make a complaint against the present prosecutor; he charged him with having stolen a 10l. note out of his pocket, at a public-house in Enfield; I understood it was on the 11th (the day before). I asked him where he got the 10l. note; he said from the auctioneer at Mr. Cook's sale, and it had been in his left hand breeches-pocket (there had been a sale a Mr. Cook's just before); I asked if he knew the number; he said he had it on a bit of paper at home, and that the auctioneer had it also in his book; I remanded the prosecutor till next morning, and then took this deposition from the prisoner - I had known him before very well - he is a labouring man, a little dealer in horses, cows, and such things; in this deposition, the nex day, he states that he had the money for a cow which he had sold. In consequence of finding that a 10l. note had not been paid to the prisoner, I discharged the prosecutor; this is his deposition - (read.)
"The loose 10l. note I received on Tuesday last of a person I don't know, for a cow which I sold for 12l. 14s. I put the whole of this money loose into my pocket; this was in the afternoon."
GEORGE DANIEL WILSON. I am a shoemaker, and live with my father, at Enfield. In September last I saw the prisoner in a public-house at Enfield, about half-past nine o'clock in the evening; I did not rob him of a 10l. note, nor take a 10l. note from him; he did not say any thing to me that night, nor make any charge against me; but about eight o'clock the next morning he sent for me; I asked what he wanted; he said, nothing, but to sit down and drink with him; about three o'clock that afternoon, he asked me to give him that piece of paper I took from him - I had not taken any thing from him of any sort of kind; he said I had taken a 10l. not from him; I told him I had not; some words ensued - he sent for a constable, and gave charge of me: I was taken to Mr. Hardy's. He said if I would give him 5l., he would pay half the expences, if I would pay the rest, and spend half a sovereign; I did not accede to that, and was kept in custody all night; next morning I was discharged: this proposal was made after I was in custody. I am a shoemaker; I am twenty-three years of age, and knew the prisoner; we always lived neighbours together; I never had any words or quarrel with him before - it was on Tuesday night, the 11th of September, that I was in the public-house with him, and on the morning of the 12th he sent for me; I sat down, and drank with him; he said nothing to me about the note then, but at three o'clock in the afternoon he sent for me again, and said I had taken a piece of paper.
GEORGE DANIEL WILSON, SEN. I am the prosecutor's father. I met the prisoner on Wednesday evening, the 12th of September, after my son was in custody - I met him accidentally in the road, and he told me my son was in the watch-house: I said, "For what?" he said for robbing him of a 10l. note the night before; but he said he did not wish to hurt him, and if I would pay him a 10l. note he would make it up, if I would pay the expences, and spend 5s. - he did not say the 10l. note, but a 10l. note; he afterwards said he supposed I had not 10l. to give him, if I would give him 5l., he would take the other by instalments, but he still proposed that I should pay the expences, and spend 5s.: the next morning we we went to the Magistrate's - he still proposed to make it up, but I would not.
COURT. Q. What were the words he used? A. He said we had better make it up, and I said, "No, I will not;" I am sure he used that expression - the talk about making it up all came from him; I had never had any dispute with him, nor had my son, to my knowledge - I do not think that I ever saw him with a 10l. note in my life.
GEORGE MEAD. I am a constable of Enfield. I took up the prosecutor, on the prisoner's charge for stealing a 10l. note.
CHARLES GREY. I am servant to Mr. Goats, a butcher, of Enfield. One Wednesday in September last, I saw the prisoner at the door of the Fallow Buck public-house, at Enfield; I saw him and another offering to sell a cow to two gentlemen in a chaise-cart, in the road, at the door - I saw them pay for it, it was in sovereigns, half sovereigns, and silver; the cow was sold for 12l. 14s. - I am accustomed to the sale of cattle; the gentleman gave the prisoner a shilling over, which he was to give to the drover, and he was to give him another shilling himself; this was a day or two before the prosecutor was taken up.
GEORGE FIELD. I was present at the sale of the cow - it was all paid for in sovereigns, half sovereigns, and silver; there was no 10l. note at all.
MARTIN NANGLE. I bought the cow of the prisoner, and paid him for it in sovereigns, half sovereigns, and silver - I am quite sure I did not pay him any 10l. note.
The prisoner put in a long written defence, stating that he had been paid for the cow in coin, but had a 10l. note in his pocket which the prosecutor had seen; that as they were drinking at the Rose and Crown public-house, the prosecutor had put his hand into his pocket, and on drawing it out, the prisoner saw his 10l. note in his hand, which he demanded, and the prosecutor refused to deliver it up then, but promised to return it presently; the prisoner suffered him to leave the house with it, as they were on friendly terms; that he called on him next morning for it, and the prosecutor promised to return it if he would go and drink at the Rose and Crown, where they went; but after many excuses, as the prosecutor did not return it, he sent for a constable, and gave him in charge.
COURT to PROSECUTOR. Q. Were you at any time in any public-house with him when you asked him to show you his money? A. No, nor did he ever show me any money; he never accused me of putting my hand into his breeches pocket, and taking out his money - we were only in one public-house that day, which was the Rose and Crown.
GUILTY - Aged 50.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18280221-168]
648. JOHN MUNROE was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of February, 28 balls of cotton, value 3s., the goods of Jonathan Orme.
JONATHAN ORME. I am a haberdasher, and live in LittleRussel-street, Covent-garden. On the 12th of February, about half-past four o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner came in for the purpose of begging charity; I had given him a waistcoat, a pair of trousers, a hat, and a shilling, three weeks before; I was engaged in serving a customer; he walked down the shop, and stood before the fire; while I was at the top of the shop I saw him walk up hastily, and he said he would call again as I was busy; I said I would attend to him directly, and asked what he wanted; he began to tell me a long tale of his distresses, and I saw under his lame arm a parcel, which I knew was my own; I took hold of him, and asked what it was; he said it was some thread and needles which he mended his clothes with - he did not wish me to see it, but I took hold of it, and it was my own.(Property produced and sworn to.)
JOHN ALDERSON. I was sent for, and took the prisoner - he took his arm out of the sling, and struck me twice in the breast; he then ran away, but was taken in the street.
Prisoner's Defence. I was intoxicated at the time.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18280221-169]
649. WILLIAM RIDLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of January, 1 coach-glass, value 6s., the goods of Thomas Pearson.
WILLIAM WARD. I am hostler to Mr. Thomas Pearson. He lost a coach-glass from the door of the coach-house, in his yard, on the 16th of January - I had seen it in the coach the same evening; I know nothing of the prisoner.
SAMUEL WRIGHT. I am beadle of St. Mary-le-bone. On the 17th of January, about two o'clock in the morning, I met the prisoner in Mortimer-street, with this glass under his arm; I stopped him, and asked where he got it; he said from a coach in a yard, opposite the Middlesex-hospital, and he was going to sell it; I said, "I suppose you stole it;" he said, "Yes, I did;" I said, "Why did you take it?" He said, "Because I was in distress."(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 32.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-170]
650. ROBERT SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of February, 1 cream-jug, value 3s., the goods of Thomas Roberts Thelluson, Esq.
JOHN BURRELL. I am in the service of Thomas Roberts Thelluson. Esq., of Glocester-place. I lost a cream-jug out of the servant's hall, about half-past eleven o'clock in the morning, on the 8th of February; the area door was unlocked, and I saw the prisoner come into the area, and soon afterwards I saw him go out of the hall; he was quite a stranger, and had no right there; I stopped him at the area gate, and he gave me this cream-jug out of his pocket.
THOMAS HOOKER. I am a constable. I was sent for, and took the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I am innocent, and never gave him the jug.
GUILTY. Aged 27.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-171]
651. HENRY JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of January, 2 guns, value 3l. 15s., and 2 gun-cases, value 5s., the goods of Benjamin Parkes.
BENJAMIN PARKES. I am a broker, and live at Turnham-green. I had two guns and two gun-cases at my door on the 19th of January; I missed them about one o'clock; I inquired about them, and about two o'clock I found them at the Hand and Flower public-house, between Kensington and Hammersmith, and knew them to be mine - I saw the prisoner coming from the Hand and Flower, and I took him into custody. I had seen him and another about my house a little before I missed them.
MARY ANN CLARK. My husband keeps the Hand and Flower. On the day in question I went into my tap-room, saw the guns there, and took them into the bar; I saw the prisoner going into the back premises - I followed him, and said, "Are you one of the prisoners? there is a cry of thieves about;" he said, "I brought the guns into the house, but the other gave me 6d. to bring them:" another person, a head and shoulders taller than himself, came in with him. I gave the same guns which they brought to the prosecutor; I believe the prisoner got out of our back premises, and got over into the road.
THOMAS PIPPEN. I met the prisoner and Matthew Webb on Hammersmith-terrace, at a quarter-past one o'clock on the 19th of January; the prisoner had a boxcoat, and Webb two guns; that was about two miles from the prosecutor's house.
JAMES PEARSON. I was down at Chiswick, and met Webb and the prisoner; the prisoner had two guns, and Webb a large coat.
JAMES RYAN. I live at the Hand and Flower. I saw Webb come in that day, with a great coat, and he called for a pint of beer; I went to get it, and when I came with it the prisoner was there, with these guns by his side.
Prisoner's Defence. I came from home to go to Ham; when I came up to the Hand and Flower, a young man asked me to carry the guns, and gave me 6d. - I never had them in my possession, but I went into the house, and they were put down; the young man then went out backwards, and this lady came in and saw the guns - I went to see for him, but could not find him.
GUILTY. Aged 15.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-172]
652. JONATHAN REYNOLDS was indicted for bigamy.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the prosecution.
THOMAS NEWSOM. I am a watchmaker, and live at Tottenham. I have known the prisoner about seven years - I was present when he was married to Mary Aldridge, at 1St. John's1 church, in St. John's-square, Clerkenwell, in 1823; I gave her away, and afterwards visited them when they were living together as man and wife; the last time I visited them was in Old-street-road, nine or ten months ago - they had three children. I saw his wife three weeks or a month ago - she was then pregnant; I saw her at a brewery in Coppice-row, which was carried on, as I understood, by the prisoner and his father-in-law.
Cross-examined by Mr. ADOLPHUS. Q. Are you any relation of the Aldridge's? A. No.; it was at the prisoner's desire that I attended to give the bride away; I knew very little of her before - I have seen her with him, and knew her name was Mary Aldridge; I never knew whether she had any other name - I do not know that I had heard her called Mary Aldridge, except when she wasmarried; I should think the prisoner was at that time about nineteen or twenty years old, and I should think her seventeen or eighteen; his parents and friends were not present - I believe it was a clandestine marriage; they did not go home to dine, but dined in Clerkenwell: the banns had been published I believe. I have been acquainted with them since; I never heard they parted by mutual consent; I have not seen the prisoner in this last brewery. but I believe he was in the former brewery, connected with Mr. Aldridge.
COURT. Q. You were at the marriage? A. Yes, and they afterwards lived together as man and wife; I know they lived together about eight months ago.
JOHN SAMUEL PENRY. I am parish-clerk of St. John, Clerkenwell. I produce the register of marriages - here is the register of October, 1823, (reads) - "Jonathan Reynolds, bachelor, and Mary Aldridge, spinster, were married in this church, by banns, this 13th of October, 1823, by me, W. E. L. Faulkener - J. Reynolds, and M. Aldridge, in the presence of Thomas Newsom and Ann Alley."
MARIA LOUISA JENNINGS. I became acquainted with the prisoner, the latter end of July last - I then lived at Albany-street, Regent's Park; he called on me for bills from the Blue Lion Brewery, in Coppice-row; we had had some beer which was returned again as not good; he called for the money, and I refused to pay for two casks; the reason I saw him was, because I settled all the accounts for Mr. Sutton - I was at that time under Mr. Sutton's protection. The prisoner represented himself to me as one of the partners in the brewery - I removed afterwards to Speldhurst-street, and the prisoner called there for the money, under an excuse that I had offered him the money in Albany-street; he first explained the objects of his visiting me, when he called one evening in September and took tea, he said a great deal of nonsense to me, which it is not necessary for me to explain; he said he was going to Bedfordshire where his guardian wished him to marry a lady of great property - he had at that time always passed as a single man; he said if he attended to the business, he could make 20l. a week by it, and if he married me he should give up a great part of his property, but did not describe what that property was; some time afterwards a groom called upon me, and brought some game, and a letter from the prisoner to which I returned an answer, that I would receive him poor or rich - three days afterwards, I saw him again - he spoke to me of the message which I had sent; he said he was unhappy in his mind, and wished to have me; he said he should give up a great part of his property; and he could have a lady directly with 20,000l.; and his father wished him to marry that lady, but he would not, for he did not like her; he said he was going to Boulogne, as he had some contract business, which he could do better there than in London; he again said he was unhappy, and if he did not have me, he should not be happy. The first time he proposed to marry me was on the 16th of October; I hesitated and said I could not think of marrying him without asking my family; I afterwards told him, my mother and my friends did not seem to like this match; he said they had nothing to do with it; if I made up my mind; and he was determined to have me; he came one morning, at eight o'clock, and said he was going immediately to Boulogne; and I told him I was about to visit a brother-in-law, Lieutenant Lawrence, who lived at No. 2, Battery, Sandown; he proposed to accompany me as far as Canterbury, and in about a fortnight afterwards we set off; he said he did not know what to do with himself, and I told him, if he could do better by going there to go, but before he got to Canterbury, he agreed to take me on to Sandown - when we arrived there, he was introduced to my brother-in-law as a lover of mine; he staid there about a week; while there, he said he must either go to Boulogne or marry me; I said he might do as he pleased, but on one Sunday morning I agreed to marry him on the Monday, but we could not get the licence till the Wednesday, when we were married at St. Peter's Sandwich, and Lieutenant Lawrence gave me away; we lived together as man and wife for one week at the Bell, at Sandwich, and then came to town to Speldhurst-street - we lived there together about a fortnight, when a Mrs. Truman called, and told me he was a married man; I challenged him with it - he said it was only some servant that he had something to say to, that he had a child by; he denied being married, and I told him I would go with him the following Sunday to this woman; he agreed to go; but while I was putting my things on to go, he said, he thought it was an improper day for me to go on such business, and I did not go, but he went with my sister the next day; I went myself on the Tuesday to his wife's house in Coppice-row; but I never saw him any more till I saw him at the office in Worship-street.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. What age are you now? Q. Twenty-three - I lived with that gentleman in Albany-street, four years and a half - I carried on his house, and kept his accounts; I had no child by him; I have had one child by a gentleman to whom I was not married; which brought me to my misfortune; he is a lieutenant in the navy; a brother officer of my sister's husband - my child is now five years old; I never had a child by an officer in the army; my mother did live at Southampton, but now lives in the city; when I removed to Spelhurst-street, I was not under the protection of the gentleman in Albany-street - I had parted with him in June - The prisoner got acquainted with me by calling a number of times I did say at one time, that whether poor or rich, I would have him; but after that he had a great deal of trouble, for I did not like his ways - my sister lived with me; but I never heard from her that he was married, and had separated from his wife.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did the prisoner know you had had a child? A. Yes; he knew all my circumstances; I told him my situation before I married him - he has not been deceived in any thing; he knew that I had an annuity of 150l. a year. I said I would have him whether poor or rich, because the servant said he was so unhappy, he was going to blow his brains out.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS, Q. But was not your annuity to be discontinued if you did an irregular thing? A. Yes; but being married was not an irregular thing - I should have retained that, though he lost his brewery.
COURT. Q. If you committed any actual irregularity, your annuity would have been forfeited; but it would have been continued if you married? A. Yes; I made himacquainted with my having this annuity, and that it would be continued if I married and lived reputably.
LIEUTENANT JAMES LAWRENCE. I am brother-in-law to Miss Jennings. I remember the prisoner coming to my house with her about the beginning of November, I think - I understood from her, in his presence, he was about to marry her, and I received him in the capacity of her intended husband - they were at my house about four or five days; he was particularly anxious to be married; he went to Canterbury to get the licence, and told me he wished Maria would make up the marriage; but she seemed backward to do so - he said his clothes were at Boulogne, and he was on his way there, but did not explain to me the reason of his going. In consequence of the desire which he expressed to me to marry her, I used my influence with her to effect it, and they were married - I was present, and gave her away.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHILL. Q. Are you married? A. Yes; my wife is living with me - she is sister to the prosecutrix - I was not aware of her situation - I had been absent from England a great deal.
GEORGE WILLIAM BENNETT. I am an officer. I apprehended the prisoner in a stable-yard at the bottom of James-street, Old-street, in the parish of St. Luke.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Confined Eighteen Months.
[trialAccount t18280221-173]
653. CHARLES WILLIS was indicted for stealing, on the 23d of January, 1 fixture, (i. e.) 1 copper, value 30s., belonging to Robert Pullman, and fixed to a certain building of his; against the Statute.
MARY FROST. I am a widow, and lodge at Mr. Pullman's - there was a copper fixed in the back kitchen of that house. On the 23d of January between six and seven o'clock in the evening, I was coming down and found something on the stairs which I thought was like the copper; I called to a woman to bring a light, and we got a brewer's servant, who went down in the back kitchen and found the prisoner; the copper had been taken from the brick-work, and put on the stairs - I had seen it safe about seven o'clock the same morning.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not meet you in the passage with the candle in your hand? A. No; I never saw you till you came up from the kitchen with Wilkins. Three families lodge in the same house - the door was open.
HENRY WILKINS. I was sent for, and went to the house; I found the prisoner on the kitchen stairs; I asked what he was doing; he made me an answer which I did not understand; and his hands were all black, as if he had been taking a copper down - the copper was then at the bottom of the stairs, I believe.
ROBERT PULLMAN. I am owner of the house - it is in West-street, St. Giles'; this copper is my property.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been taking up some old floors at the west end of the town; I was returning home, and was taken ill; I had no half-pence to go to a public-house, and seeing the door of this house open, I went into the yard - this woman accosted me when I came back, and asked what I wanted there; I told her, and she said she thought I had pulled down a copper; I said I had not, and she went to see - I did not attempt to go away; if I had pulled it down I must have had some tool about me.
JURY to MR. PULLMAN. Q. Could the copper have been removed without any tools? A. Yes; it could have been pulled up by the hands.
GUILTY. Aged 32.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-174]
654. CHARLES WESTON was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of January, 1 1/2 peck of wheat, value 2s., the goods of Thomas Haynes.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the prosecution.
THOMAS HAYNES. I am a little farmer, and live at Staines, Middlesex; the prisoner was in my employ. On the 4th of January he was employed in thrashing wheat; I saw him come and open the barn door and let himself in- he had a little basket with a bit of victuals in it, I believe; about five o'clock he was leaving his work; I had been on the look out, and was then behind the door; I saw he had the same basket, and it looked very bulky; I called him back and said "What have you got;" he said "Nothing master, I have not indeed;" I said I would insist upon seeing - he sprung two yards from me - I sprung after him, took his basket, and found in it about a peck and a half of clean wheat; he said "Pray master forgive me - this is the first time;" after I took it, he got away, as I had no one to assist me; he was afterwards taken at Kingston - he lived at a place called Thorp, about two miles from Staines.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. How many persons were about that spot? A. Only one man who worked with the prisoner; he is not here - I have no doubt that this is a part of my wheat.
GEORGE LAYTON. I am a constable. I received information, and went to the prisoner's house two days afterwards, but could not find him; I gave information to a person who brought him from Kingston, in about three weeks afterwards, and gave him into my custody.
Prisoner's Defence. I gleaned it in the summer after my labour; I carried it home, and thrashed it out at my leisure; I took this wheat out to a mill to be ground, but there was no one there; I took it to the barn and left it with my clothes; when I went out with it my master said, "What have you got here?" I said, "Nothing of yours" - he took the basket down and said, "This is a pretty thing, is it not - don't shew your face here, I will hinder your working here, or any where else any more." I stopped to speak to a person, and my master went on - the next morning I heard of a job at Kingston, and I went there; my master had no wheat in that barn; I never said,"Master forgive me."
GUILTY. Aged 35.
Confined Three Months, and Publicly Whipped at Staines on a Market Day.
[trialAccount t18280221-175]
655. SUSANNAH TUBBS and SARAH PHILLIPS were indicted for stealing, on the 23d of January, 2 teapots, value 2s.; 1 glass, value 6d.; 4 images, value 2s.; 1 pair of drawers, value 1d., and 1 brush, value 6d., the goods of Richard Warren.
ELIZABETH WARREN. I am the wife of Richard Warren - I do not know where he lives now, but I live in Carlisle-street, Bethnal-green; my husband is a weaver, and I am a weaveress. I lost all the articles stated, on the 23d of January, from the parlour in my house; I was athome and up stairs - my parlour door was not locked: the prisoners were neighbours, when we lived in the country.
ROBERT MAY. I am a weaver. On the 23d of January I met the two prisoners about a quarter of a mile from the prosecutrix's house - I had seen them both before, and knew Phillips very well: I saw the spout of a tea-pot, which I knew was Warren's, hanging out of Tubbs' apron - I stopped them, and took them back to Mrs. Warren's.
GEORGE PAYNE. I was in Carlisle-street about half-past twelve o'clock on the 23d of January; May came out of the house, and asked me if I had seen any women come out - I said Yes; I had seen Phillips come out, and Tubbs stood outside - I told him if he went towards Hare-street he would find them.
JAMES HANLEY. I am an officer, and took the prisoners the same night.(Property produced and sworn to.)
The prisoners put in a written defence, stating that they had called on the prosecutrix, and finding her from the room, had taken the property in a joke, to alarm her, and that she had since taken 10s. to forego the prosecution.
COURT to ELIZABETH WARREN. Q. Is there any pretence for charging you with taking any money? A. No - I never permitted them to take my property. I never knew they were near the place.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280221-176]
656. JOSIAH JAMES HOSIER was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of February, 2 bushels of coals, value 2s., the goods of William Yool, in a certain barge upon the Navigable River Thames .
JOSEPH WILSON. I know Mr. William Yool - he is a coal-merchant. On the 16th of February, a little before two o'clock in the afternoon, I was going down in the craft on the river, and saw the Edward barge laying outside the dock of Brooks and Penny's wharf; I am their clerk. I saw the prisoner's head just over the barge, which was loaded with coals; he was taking coals out of the barge, into his boat; I came up to him, and he said, "If you will let me go, I will put them back," and he did throw two or three pieces back; I took hold of the chain of his boat, and held him till an officer came - I suppose there were nearly two bushels of coals in the boat- they appeared the same sort as those in the barge.
ALEXANDER MITCHELL. I am an officer. I was sent for, to take the prisoner; Wilson was standing on the gunwale of the barge Edward, and had hold of the chain of the boat, in which there was about a bushel of small coals and two bushels of large coals; they appeared to me to be the same sort as those in the barge.
CHRISTOPHER BEARPARK. I am wharf-clerk to Mr. William Yool. I went to the boat, and found the coals in it; they appeared to me to be part of the bulk of the barge Edward.
COURT to ALEXANDER MITCHELL. Q. Did you hear the prisoner examined? A. Yes - this deposition was read over to him; this is Mr. Broderip's signature to it; there was no promise or threat made to the prisoner - (read).
"I have been led into it, by keeping company with a man whom the officers know to be a noted thief."
The prisoner put in a written defence, standing that the coals were only the sweepings of the barge. He received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18280221-177]
657. JANE ATKINS was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of February, 2 pots, value 1s. 6d., the goods of Joseph Bennett; and 1 pot, value 1s. 6d., the goods of George Hamble.
WILLIAM HAYES. I am a tailor. On the 9th of February I saw the prisoner come and take two pots from off the rails, next door to me; I detained her, and sent for Mr. Bennett.
JOSEPH BENNETT. I am a publican. Mr. Hayes sent for me; I found these two pots, which are my property, in a bag on the prisoner.
GEORGE HAMBLE. I keep the Northumberland Arms public-house, Tottenham-court-road . This quart pot, which was found on the prisoner, is mine.
GUILTY. Aged 54.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18280221-178]
658. JOHN ATKINSON and JAMES MASON were indicted for stealing, on the 29th of December, 5lbs. weight of pork, value 3s., the goods of Thomas Moorman.
THOMAS MOORMAN. I keep a cheesemonger's-shop, in Golden-lane. On the 29th of December, about a quarter before nine o'clock in the evening, I saw three men pass my shop, turn the corner, and make a stop; I told my boy to look out, and in about three minutes the boy said, "Halloo, master - what have you got at?" a piece of pork was then gone; I saw Mason running away: the boy pursued him, and cried Stop thief! I saw two men run from the place where they had all stopped; I did not see Atkinson when Mason ran away; but I had seen them together about three minutes before, and am quite certain of their persons; neither of them were taken that night.
WILLIAM MOORMAN. I was at the window, and saw both the prisoners pass, in company together; in about three minutes afterwards I saw Mason take a piece of pork from the window; I gave the alarm, but they ran away - I had not seen them before, but am quite certain of their persons - I knew them again the moment they were taken up, and am quite sure of them.
JOHN TWEEDY. I am a constable. I took the prisoners on the night of the 16th of January, from the description of the prosecutor; the lad Moorman came up in a moment, and said, "These are the men that took the pork away."
JAMES BUCKLE. I was with Tweedy; the lad pointed out the prisoners - I had heard of the robbery on the night it happened, and the prisoners' persons were described to me, but I had to go into the country on account of the death of my father, and therefore they were not taken.
ATKINSON's Defence. The boy said he believed I was one of them, and he goes by what his father said; the Magistrate said he would have the father locked up for telling the boy what to say.
MASON's Defence. I have lived in the neighbourhood; the prosecutor knew me very well, and saw me several times after it was done.
THOMAS MOORMAN. I saw him once on the 11th of January, and went to try to take him, but he ran away.
ATKINSON - GUILTY. Aged 22.
MASON - GUILTY. Aged 21.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-179]
659. RICHARD BLANKS was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of January, 1 cart load of gravel, value 1s. 6d., the goods of the Surveyors of the highways, for the time being, of St. John, at Hackney .
The prisoner pleaded GUILTY

660. JAMES BLINE was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of February, 11 yards of printed cotton, value 11s., the goods of Thomas Fowke.
WILLIAM POOK. I am in the service of Thomas Fowke, a linen-draper, of Tottenham-court-road. The prisoner came there on the 7th of February, and asked if we wanted to purchase any silk handkerchiefs; I said No, and he went away - after he was gone we missed a piece of print from the counter; I pursued, and overtook him about a quarter of a mile from the shop; I asked if he had been in Mr. Fowke's shop; he said he knew no such person - I let him go on, but Green, one of Mr. Fowke's men came up, and in consequence of what he said, I went to the prisoner again; I said, "I think you are the man - let me look into your hat," which he did, but there was nothing: I then took the corner of the handkerchief from the bundle under his arm, and there I saw this print, which I know to be my master's.
GUILTY. Aged 34.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-181]
661. CHARLES BOYD was indicted for stealing, on 16th of January, 1 roll of cotton padding, value 9s., the goods of William Law.
PHOEBE LAW. I am the wife of William Law, and keep a trimming-shop. On the 16th of January, about half-past six o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came to the shop, took this cotton, and ran off with it; there was no person in the shop - I pursued him down Cecil-court, St. Martin's-court, and into Hop-gardens; a gentleman gave me the cotton, and I returned; the prisoner was brought back in about three minutes - I am certain he is the man; I had seen him watching for half an hour before he took the cotton.
Prisoner. Q. What can you swear to me by? A. By seeing you on the Saturday night before, in the pouring rain; and then for half an hour before you came in the shop - I saw you and a boy watching me through the window, and I was watching you as well.
SAMUEL SHUTTLEWORTH. I am a labourer. I had been to Mr. Baron Garrow's, to whom my wife is laundress - I was passing St. Martin's-court, heard the alarm, and saw the prisoner running; I pursued him, and he dropped this piece of cotton from under his right arm - I never lost sight of him - he was taken in Hop-gardens.
JAMES ASHLEY. I am an officer, and took the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-182]
662. EDWIN BIRD was indicted for feloniously marrying Elizabeth Skipp, his former wife, Emma Bellet Bondin, being still alive.
MATILDA AVILA. I was present at Stepney church when the prisoner was married to Emma Bellet Bowden - her name was not Bondin.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280221-183]
663. THOMAS BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of February, 1 pair of shoes, value 8s., the goods of Richard Chapman.
PHILIP KIMBELL. I am a watchman. On the 15th of February I was calling the hour of nine in the evening, and saw the prisoner passing Mr. Chapman's shop, in Wood-street; I did not like his proceedings, and I retired to a small court; I saw him take these shoes from the window- he ran towards me; I caught him, and took them from him; I took him to the shop: Mr. Chapman was not at home, and I took him to the watch-house.
RICHARD CHAPMAN. These are my shoes - I know nothing of the prisoner.
Prisoner. It is the first time I was ever under any charg.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 25.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18280221-184]
664. ROBERT BARRELL was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of January, 73 books, value 3l., the goods of William Tucker.
WILLIAM TUCKER. I am a schoolmaster, and live in Thurlow-place, Hackney-road. My school room is at the bottom of my garden, detached from my house; I missed some books from there on the 24th of January - I have found seventy-three of them, but I lost more.
LEWIS MYERSON. I am an officer. The prosecutor applied to me, and I went to Garrard's, where I found about thirty books; and then to Davies', where I found forty more.
JOHN GARRARD. I am a general salesman, and deal in clothes and other things - I live at No. 10, Hackney-road. I bought these books of Mr. Cohen, in the Kingsland-road; about twelve or one o'clock on the day stated.
LEWIS COHEN. I live at No. 196, Kingsland-road - I keep a clothes shop, and deal in every thing - it is not what they call a marine-store shop. I sold these books to Garrard on the 24th of January - I bought them of the prisoner the same day for 8s., and sold them for 12s.; I think there were thirty of them: the prisoner called first, and asked if I would buy them - I told him to call in an hour; I knew him before, he has bought shoes and other things of me; I then went to Garrard's, and asked if he would buy them - he said, Yes; I then bought them, and took them to him: I did not ask the prisoner where he got them - he said he was a carpenter; I did not think it odd that he should have thirty books for sale - I do not in general sell books; I had not seen them at the time - I went to Garrard's, and asked him if he would buy them of me, if I bought them of a man: the prisoner told me they were only school books, but I did not know of what description, and could not describe them; 8s. is not very cheap - some of them are no more than waste paper - nothing had been said about the price I was to give for them, before I went to Garrard; I then went and asked if he would buy them.
JAMES HILL. I am a headborough. I have some of the books which were taken at Garrard's.
MR. TUCKER. The whole of these books are my property.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHILL. Q. Have not your boys the care of their own books? A. They have while they are at school; but when they are not I consider them as under my care.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-185]
665. CATHERINE CONNOR was indicted for stealing on the 9th of January, 1 waistcoat, value 2s.; 1 pair of boots, value 6s., and 1 piece of calico, value 1/2d., the goods of Thomas Pattison.
MARY PATTISON. I am the wife of John Pattison - he lives in Dogget-court, Moorfields; I am an upholsteress. On the 31st of December, the prisoner came to assist me, as I was ill - she continued there, and in a few days I missed this waistcoat; I asked her about it, and she said she had seen one, and brought me an old one - two or three days afterwards I missed some other things, and these boots; I taxed her with it, and she brought me the duplicate of the boots - I never allowed her to pawn any thing.
THOMAS PATTISON. I went with the duplicate, and got these boots and this waistcoat, which are mine.
EDWARD PERKINS. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Whitecross-street. I took these boots and waistcoat of the prisoner on the 9th of January.
Prisoner's Defence. She was very often sending me to pawn things.
GUILTY. Aged 53.
Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18280221-186]
666. RICHARD DENMAN and WILLIAM JILKS were indicted for stealing, on the 26th of January, 2 loaves of bread, value 1s. 6d., the goods of James Scott.
JAMES MCINTOSH. I am a street-keeper. On the 26th of January, I saw a basket of bread on a wheelbarrow, in Devonshire-street, Mary-le-bone - I saw the two prisoners together, and Denman took two loaves out, which he put into an apron - Jilks was looking at him; I followed them to Harley-street, and there took them; Denman said he took them out of a baker's basket.
JAMES SCOTT. I am a baker. I left my basket of bread in Devonshire-street - I went to deliver some loaves; the officer came, and asked me if I had lost any loaves - I looked, and missed two.
DENMAN's Defence. I had been out of work, and did it for want.
DENMAN - GUILTY. Aged 27.
JILKS - GUILTY. Aged 20.
Confined Three Weeks.
[trialAccount t18280221-187]
667. DAVID DAVY, JOHN HUMPHREYS, and WILLIAM BRANDON were indicted for stealing, on the 17th of February, 1 veil, value 2s., the goods of George Tolfree.
HARRIET TOLFREE. I am the daughter of George Tolfree - he keeps a general clothes-shop in Upper Whitecross-street. On the 17th of February, about a quarter-past seven o'clock, the prisoners came to our shop - Brandon bought some bread and butter, and as soon as they were gone I saw the wire work of the window was open, which had been shut when they came in, and I missed a white lace veil from the window - I went to the door, and saw the prisoners on the other side of the way; Mr. Steel and I went over to them - I asked them for the veil, they denied having it, but Davy dropped it - we took him, he had stood close to the wire in the shop.
WILLIAM STEEL. I live at the house. I was going home, and saw the prisoner in the shop - Tolfree called me from the back room - I went and took Davy, who dropped the veil.
THOMAS BROAD. I am a watchman. I was called, and took Davy to the watch-house, with the veil, and next morning I took the others.(Property produced and sworn to.)
BRANDON's Defence. I went to the shop, but I did not take the veil; while I was paying for what I had, the other two boys went out and I followed them - I did not know they had any thing.
DAVY - GUILTY. Aged 12.
HUMPHREYS - GUILTY. Aged 13.
BRANDON - GUILTY. Aged 13.
Whipped and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18280221-188]
668. THOMAS ELLIOTT was indicted for stealing, on the 15th February, 1 keg, value 1s., and 10 quarts of a certain compound spirit, called cloves, value 20s., the goods of Ann Hawkins Swaine and others.
WILLIAM KENDRICK. I am a publican, and live in Sutton-street. On the 15th of February I was lodging at the Horse and Groom public-house, St. John's-street, Clerkenwell - I was looking out of the window between two and three o'clock, and saw a cart loaded near the door - the prisoner came and stood by the side, and another person who was with him got on the wheel, took a keg out of the cart, and gave it to the prisoner, who walked down Jerusalem-passage with it - he had an apron on, and looked like a porter; I pursued and brought him back with the keg - I gave him into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Then the other seemed to employ the prisoner? A. They came together up to the cart - the other man took the keg from the cart.
COURT. Q. How was the other man dressed? A. I think he had a blue coat, a black silk handkerchief, and a white hat.
WILLIAM BALLS. I am carman to Swaine, Allport and Co. - Ann Hawkins Swaine is one of the partners - they are distillers, and live at Holborn-bridge. I was to take this keg of cloves to the Borough - I had some goods to deliver in St. John-street, and left my cart at the public-house door for two or three minutes; I am sure I was not absent more; and during that time the keg was gone - I did not see the prisoner till he was brought back with it.
JOHN ROBINSON. I am an officer. I was coming down St. John's-square, and one or two persons were bringing the prisoner down - I took charge of him.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was looking for work, and was met by the man, who asked if I wanted a job - I said, Yes; he said he had a keg to take to Spa-fields.
WILLIAM KENDRICK. The other man was not with him when I took him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not look round as if to look for the other man? A. He did not say any thing to me - he might look round.
GUILTY. Aged 22.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-189]
669. MARY ELLCOCK was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of February, 1 sheet, value 3s., and 1 blanket, value 4s, the goods of Maria Davenport.
MARIA DAVENPORT. I am single, and live in Turnmill-street. On the 2nd of February, I let a ready-furnished lodging to the prisoner - she ran away at eight o'clock in the morning on the 5th of February; the articles stated were let to her with the lodgings - she passed as a married woman, but at the watch-house she said she was not married - she gave me no notice: her husband came down the morning she left, and told me his wife had robbed me of a blanket - I went and looked, and missed the sheet and blanket likewise; he went after her; she came to me on the Friday evening following, and said she had pawned them, and would redeem them, and bring them back - I said, I did not wish to hurt her; I believed then that she was married, but when at the watch-house she said she was not married, and could not take the man's name - she put down Mary Ann Smith there.
WILLIAM CLARK. I am an officer. The prisoner was brought to the watch-house - I found the duplicates on her, and she told me where they were pawned.
THOMAS REDPATH. I am a pawnbroker. I have a blanket pawned by the prisoner.
HENRY PARKER. I am a pawnbroker. I have a sheet pawned by the prisoner on the 8th of February.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. My husband and I both took the room on the Friday; I had 18s. to pay, and I pawned those things, meaning to redeem them; I had no intention of stealing them - my husband is, I believe, living there still; I did not return for fear of being ill used.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280221-190]
670. MARY ELLCOCK was again indicted for stealing on the 9th of February, 1 tea-pot, value 3s., the goods of Mary Ann Humbley.
MARY ANN HUMBLEY. I lodge in Turnmill-street. I lost my tea-pot, on the 9th of February, out of the back kitchen - the prisoner lodged in the house with her husband; I believe I missed my tea-pot on the Sunday after the prisoner was taken into custody - I saw it at Hatton-garden.
THOMAS REDPATH. I am a pawnbroker. I have a tea-pot, pawned on the 7th of February by the prisoner for 2s. 6d.
Prisoner's Defence. The tea-pot was on the kitchen chimney - I took it with an intention of getting the sheet out, but I was afraid to go home, and was obliged to break into the money.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280221-191]
Fifth Middlesex Jury - before Mr. Common Sergeant.
671. HENRY GIBBS was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of February, 8 lbs, weight of tea, value 2l.; 3 lbs. weight of coffee, value 5s., and 9 lbs. weight of sugar, value 7s., the goods of Edward Davis.
JAMES JONES. I am a porter in the employ of Edward Davis, a grocer, of Philpot-lane. On the 8th of February, I was sent out, about three o'clock, with the horse and cart, and some tea and coffee in it - about half past four o'clock, I was in Verulam-buildings, Gray's-inn-lane; I left my horse and cart at the door of No. 4, while I went in to take some parcels - I went over to the Talbot public-house to get change; when I came back, the prisoner was coming up to the cart, with a man on each side of him, with some parcels of tea, sugar, and coffee - there were names on the parcels; I believe one was Stuckey, and one Bean - I am sure they were safe in my cart when I left it.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. How long were you away? A. Five or six minutes. I did not stay at the Talbot - I had not seen the prisoner near the cart.
THOMAS DAVIES. I am in the employ of the prosecutor. I sent out the porter with these parcels; I did not write on them, but I saw that they were written on.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know the writing on them? A. No. I have compared these parcels with the book, and the bills of parcels, but they are not here.
COURT. Q. Were they parcels like these? A. Yes; I see no difference.
JOHN JONES. I am a painter. I was at work in Verulam-buildings; I saw the porter come there, and leave the cart - I directed him to go to the Talbot for change; I saw the prisoner close to the shaft of the cart, and another man on the shaft; be took the parcels out and gave them to the prisoner, who ran away with them; I pursued and stopped him with them, in the gateway, leading to Gray's Inn - I had not lost sight of him: I took him back to the cart, and gave the parcels to the carman.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it a covered cart? A. Yes; the man on the shaft delivered the parcels to the prisoner. I cannot say whether they came together; the prisoner went away at a swift walk, at a brisk pace; he said he was employed by the man who belonged to the cart - he came back without resistance: he carried them in his arms uncovered.
COURT. Q. Did he say where he was directed to carry them? A. No. I took him within ten minutes.
WILLIAM WEALAND. I am a painter. I was with Jones and went in pursuit of the prisoner; we took him with three or four parcels on him, and brought him back to Verulam-buildings.
WILLIAM BROWN EDWARDS. I am an officer. I took the prisoner, and bring the parcels.
Prisoner's Defence. The witness states that I was at the side of the cart, which I was not - the man in the cart dropped one parcel, and I took it up; he said "Will you have the goodness to take these parcels to the lodge in Gray's Inn." I took them, and he went on before me; he ran on, and then I went quicker, till the witnesses came; I said "If you ran through the lodge, you will see the man;" and they took me back to the cart.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 39.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18280221-192]
672. JOSEPH GOBBEY, WILLIAM BACHELOR, and THOMAS BALDICK were indicted for stealing, onthe 11th of January, 50 pieces of wood, value 5s., the goods of Samuel Puddefoot.
SAMUEL PUDDEFOOT, SEN. I am a labourer and live at Finchley, in Middlesex. On the 11th of January, I lost some pieces of wood, which we make from felling timber trees, on the premises of Mr. Fenning, at Manor-house Farm; I had felled the timber, and the wood I made in felling belongs to me; it laid in small heaps on the field - I met the three prisoners on the road, about a quarter of a mile from the field, with each a full sack, but I do not know what was in them; I asked what they had got, and Gobbey said they had got some wood; I told him they had been into Mr. Fenning's field, they said they knew they had; I said it was my property; they offered to carry it to my house if I would settle it - I would not; they said they thought the wood belonged to the gentlemen of the ground; if they had known it was mine, they would not have taken it. I left them and went on to my son; who had been left to watch the wood.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. When you left them, where did they go? A. They went home. I took out a summons for them on the Monday, and they were before the Magistrates on the Friday; I have not got the chips here; they took them home I suppose; I never saw them at all - they would have been worth 9s. if they were sacks of chips: the Magistrate did not demand of these men 14s. a sack; or say they should go to the tread-mill for a month; he said 3s. a sack, but the whole expense came to 14s. 3d.; he did not say he would send them to the tread-mill in my presence; their families have gone to the parish since.
SAMUEL PUDDEFOOT. I am a labourer. I saw these men come to the field, and fill the sacks with my father's wood; I did not speak to them; I thought I had better not go near them.
Cross-examined. Q. How far were you from them? A. About twenty-five yards; they could not see me, it was very dark; it was about five o'clock; I could see them, because I was watching - I dare say they could have seen me if they had looked: I followed them, when they were going off with the wood, but I was not near them when my father met them - I did not go off the premises; I was not near enough to distinguish their faces, nor the colour of their clothes, but I saw three men filling sacks.
COURT. Q. How do you know that these were the men? A. By my following them, and my father meeting them directly - I did not see him meet them: if my father had not met them, I should not have known it was them.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280221-193]
673. HENRY GARNSEY was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of January, 11lbs. of bacon, value 3s., the goods of Richard Ward.
SAMUEL HIND. I am superintendant of the business of Mr. Richard Ward, of Kingsland-road. On the 16th of January my boy called me - I ran out, pursued the prisoner, and took this bacon from him - it had been on some baskets, about two feet within the shop; he said he was very sorry, and hoped I would forgive him.
JOHN WILLIAM WINTLE. I am headborough. I was sent for, and took the prisoner.
JAMES CHANDLER. I was in the shop, and saw the prisoner come and take the bacon.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I am very sorry - I had not a halfpenny - it is the first time.
GUILTY. Aged 68.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
Fined 1s. and Discharged.

674. JAMES HENREHAN was indicted for stealing, on the 22d of December, 1 watch-chain, value 4s.; 2 seals, value 7s., and 1 eye-glass, value 1s. 6d., the goods of Thomas William Andrews.
THOMAS WILLIAM ANDREWS. I am shopman to Messrs. Neats, pawnbrokers. On the 22d of December their house was under repair; on which day I missed these articles, and other things, out of a box, in the sale-room, which was then under repair; I had seen them safe about ten days before.
JOHN PERRY. I am shopman to a silversmith, of Oxford-street. On the 22d of December the prisoner brought a silver chain, a seal, and an eye-glass, to sell; I asked whose property they were - he said they were "bestowed" upon him - he produced them singly - I gave him 1s. for the eye-glass, and 3s. 4d. for the seal and chain.
ANTHONY LAW. I am apprentice at the shop which Perry lives at. On the night of the 22d of December I bought a gold seal of the prisoner for 6s.
THOMAS HOOKER. I am an officer, and received the prisoner in charge.
GEORGE THOMAS KEYS. The prisoner was employed in repairing Mr. Neat's house, on the 22d of December - he always appeared a steady man.
Prisoner's Defence. The rubbish was being put into the cart - I found these things among it.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18280221-195]
675. THOMAS HOLLIDAY was indicted for stealing, on the 22d of January, 10 lbs. of bacon, value 7s., the goods of John Burden.
JOHN BURDEN, JUN. My father, John Burden, keeps a chandler's-shop, in Little Guildford-street. On the 22d of January I was in the parlour, and heard footsteps - I ran out, and was directed up the street, but could not find any one; and while I was telling the watchman the prisoner came by the shop, with this bacon under his arm, and was taken - he did not say how he got it.
THOMAS SAINT. I am a watchman. I stopped the prisoner with the bacon - he ran when he saw me - he afterwards said he did it through distress; I told him I knew better, as he had been in a good situation.
JAMES CHANDLER. I was at the Red Lior public-house, Little Guildford-street. The prisoner came into the taproom, and offered this bacon for sale.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Confined Two Months.

676. MATTHEW HILL was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of February, 2 waistcoats, value 3s.; 1 pair of trousers, value 3s., and 1 bason, value 1s., the goods of Thomas Curtis.
THOMAS CURTIS. I am apprentice to Mr. Le Grose, a watchmaker. On the 10th of February, about ten o'clock in the morning, my clothes were safe in my box, in the kitchen, and I saw the prisoner looking through the window - I went out, and on returning, at night, missed them;next morning I went to No. 78, St. Martin's-lane, where the prisoner is apprenticed, and found them in his box - his parents live at my master's.
EDWARD ADAMS. I am a constable. I went and took the prisoner, at his master's house - he said he was sorry for it, and hoped he should be forgiven; he took me to his box, and gave the property to me.(Property produced and sworn to.)

677. JOHN LEARY and MICHAEL COLLINS were indicted for stealing, on the 12th of February, 10 yards of rope, value 2s., the goods of William Smith.
SAMUEL SMITH. I am son of William Smith, a carrier. I was in Whitechapel, about nine o'clock in the morning, with his cart, and missed this rope - I had seen the prisoners about Whitechapel.
JAMES FOWLER. I am an officer. On the 12th of February I was in Whitechapel, and saw Leary take the rope from the cart, and give it to Collins - we secured them.
FRANCIS KEYS. I was with Fowler. I saw Leary creep under the cart, take the rope, and throw it to Collins.
LEARY's Defence. I asked this boy where I could sell it - I have no friends.
COLLINS' Defence. He asked me where he could sell it- I was not near the cart.
LEARY - GUILTY. Aged 14.
COLLINS - GUILTY. Aged 11.
