678. JOHN MARCH was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of January, a pair of stays, value 3s, the goods of Sarah Moss.
SARAH MOSS. I am a stay-maker, and live in Prospect-row, Kingsland. On the 15th of January I was in the parlour, I heard a noise, came into the shop, and saw the prisoner about two doors off, with something in his apron - I missed the stays from the window; I was too ill to follow him, and he got away; I am sure he is the boy - I saw him again on the Saturday.
ANN SARAH BARLOW. The prisoner came to me on the morning of the 15th, to my shop, which is a quarter of a mile from Moss' - he asked the price of tulip roots - I told him 4d. each - he went out, but came in soon after, and took a hyacinth glass from the window - I took it from him, and found these stays in his apron; he said he brought them from his aunt's, at Haggerston - I gave him in charge.
GEORGE SMITH. I took him in charge, with the stays, which have the shop ticket on them.
GUILTY. Aged 12.
Recommended to Mercy. - Whipped and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18280221-199]
679. ELIZA NORMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of December, 1 gown, value 5s., the goods of William Willis.
CAROLINE THOMPSON. I am William Willis' daughter-in-law - he lives in Stingo-yard. On the 11th of December the prisoner came and asked if Mrs. Thompson lived there; I said Yes, as that was my mother's former name; she asked me to show her the privy, which I did - she said she did not like to go there, and asked to go up stairs - I took her up; I stood on the landing-place - she pointed to my room, and asked if I slept there; she was scratching her leg, and said it itched, and I think she was then taking my mother's gown, for there was nobody there but her - it was a chocolate colour, with a chintz flower; she was gone before I missed it; I told Mr.Willis, when he came home, and in about two months I saw the prisoner in the street, and told my mother that was the woman - she sent for an officer, and he took her; I had seen the gown about an hour before she came.
MARY ANN BASS. On the 11th of December I was standing at my mother's door, No. 2, Stingo-yard. The prisoner came and asked if I knew Mrs. Thompson, who kept a mangle - I said No, but a neighbour directed her to Willis' - the prisoner knocked at their door, and went in, and in about half an hour my mother sent me to Mr. Jenks', a pawnbroker, and while I was there the prisoner came in with Mrs. Willis' gown, which she laid on the counter, and asked 3s. on it - they would only lend 2s. 6d. - I said to her, "You found it out?" she said Yes - I said, "But she did not keep a mangle?" she said No, it was her mistake; that it was all right, and thanked me - I had seen Mrs. Willis wear the gown.
JOHN EDWARD BENNETT. I live at Mr. Jenks'. The prisoner came and offered a chocolate gown, with a chintz flower - I would not lend her 3s., and she took it away.
THOMAS HENRY THOMPSON. I am a Bow-street patrol. On the 16th of February, in consequence of information, I went to No. 4, Stephen-street, and found the prisoner; she said she was the most unlucky girl in the world, and offered to pay for the gown rather than be locked up.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going for something for dinner, when this child accused me of taking the gown; I never saw her in my life, and was never in the place; I took no gown to the pawnbroker's.
GUILTY. Aged 22.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-200]
680. JAMES O'NEAL was indicted for stealing, on the 2d of February, 1 set of harness, value 2/., and 1 brush, value 6d., the goods of Joseph Duck.
BAILEY DUCK. I am the son of Joseph Duck, of Plaistow. On the 2d of February he hired the prisoner to mend this harness; and next day two officers came, and produced it cut into pieces, and quite destroyed.
JOSEPH NEWSOM. I am an officer. On the 2d of February, about half-past seven o'clock, I met the prisoner, with these pieces of harness, in Whitechapel-road; he was putting them under his coat; I asked where he got them; he said he bought them in Rosemary-lane: I took him to a public-house; and Tyrrell found the rest of the harness round his body, and inside his hat.
ROBERT TYRRELL. I was with Newsom, and found this harness bound round the prisoner's body; I found "J. Duck, Plaistow," cut on one of the pieces, and went there.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I went to Rosemary-lane to buy some old harness to make it up again.
GUILTY. Aged 30.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18280221-201]
681. MARIA PARKER was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of February, 1 blanket, value 2s.; 1 pillow,value 1s., and 1 coverlid, value 1s., the goods of William Bartlam.
MARY ANN BARTLAM. I am the wife of William Bartlam; we live on Saffron-hill; the prisoner came to lodge with us three weeks ago, and left on the 8th of February; I missed these articles, and several more from her room.
JOHN FITCH. I am a pawnbroker; the prisoner pawned a pillow and blanket with me.
EDWARD JOSEPH BURCHELL. I am superintendant at the Refuge for the Houseless; the prisoner came there; I received information, searched her, and found some duplicates of this property on her.
SAMUEL HAMPSTEAD. I am a pawnbroker; a woman pawned this quilt with me.
GEORGE WILMOT. I received the prisoner in charge; she said she was sorry for what she had done.
The prisoner pleaded distress.
GUILTY. Aged 45.
Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18280221-202]
682. JAMES WILLIAM PEAT was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of February, 1 hat, value 5s., and 1 pair of gloves, value 3d., the goods of Richard Perry.
RICHARD PERRY. I am a boot-maker. On the 6th of February, I was in my shop, in Bath-street; a person gave me information, and I missed my hat and gloves: I went out, and overtook a boy - I found my hat and gloves in a house.
MARY ANN CRAWLEY. The prisoner came into Mrs. Chapman's shop, and asked if he might leave a hat and gloves, and two blacking-bottles there; he left them on the counter - the bat was put under the counter.
JOSEPH HASSAY. The prisoner came into my shop, about four o'clock in the afternoon, with two bottles of blacking, and in about half an hour I saw him in Perry's custody; I live about three minutes walk from Chapman's.
CHRISTOPHER SHOILS. I saw a boy go into Perry's shop with the blacking to sell - I live opposite, and saw him run out with a hat in his hand; I went to the door, and saw him come out of Chapman's shop without anything - I went and told Perry.
SARAH CHAPMAN. A boy came to my shop, bought a penny-worth of sweetmeats, and asked if he might leave the hat, gloves, and blacking - I cannot swear to the prisoner.
ALEXANDER MOON. I took the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I left nothing in that shop.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 14.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Seven Days.
[trialAccount t18280221-203]
683. GEORGE KNIGHT was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of January, 6 bottles of wine, value 9s., the goods of Andrew Leonard Jacks.
WILLIAM JONES. I saw the prisoner bottling some wine, belonging to the doctor on board the Tender, at the Tower-stairs - he put six bottles into his own locker, and locked it- he is ward-room steward; I told nobody of it for a day or two - the first Lieutenant and Master were on board.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is the doctor here? A. No; he told the prisoner to bottle off the wine; he did not say it was his wine - it was kept in the gun-room- that is not the doctor's room; the prisoner was caterer of the mess. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280221-204]
684. JOHN PARSONS was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of November, 1 potatoe-fork, value 2s., the goods of Thomas Wallis.
THOMAS WALLIS. I live at Tottenham. I lent this fork to Deller about a week before it was lost - I know it to be mine.
DANIEL DELLER. I live at the Ship public-house, Wallis lent me this fork; I laid it on the rack, in the stable, on the 17th or 18th of November; I saw it safe on the evening of the 18th, and on the morning of the 19th it was gone; the prisoner used to sleep in the hay-loft - I know the ostler, and he allowed me to leave it there; I saw the prisoner there the night it was stolen.
JOHN CASTLE. I live at Tottenham. On Monday, the 19th of November, about half-past five o'clock, I saw the prisoner as I came out of my house to work - he had this fork under his arm, and asked me to buy it; I said No, but I bought it at last, and kept it till the officer came with a warrant to the farm I work at, and took it.
JOSEPH FORSTER. I am an officer, and took the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not sleep there; on the morning I sold him the fork I found it under a hedge, in the field, and did not know it had been in the loft; a witness knows I was not there, but I could not afford to pay him to come.
GUILTY. Aged 26.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-205]
685. FRANCIS SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of November, 1 watch, value 4l.; 1 ribbon, value 1d., and 1 key, value 1d., the goods of Elizabeth Marrs.
ELIZABETH MARRS. On the 26th of November, about four o'clock in the afternoon, I lost a watch, which hung in my parlour, in Lisson-grove; three boys came into the shop; I got up to shut the door, after they were gone, and missed it - they came first in the morning; the prisoner was with them then, but I cannot swear he came in the afternoon.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You have convicted three other boys? A. Only two were convicted - three were tried.
GEORGE TEASDALE BUCKER. I am a pawnbroker, and live at Brentford. This watch was pawned in the name of Daniel Cooper; I believe, by the prisoner.
THOMAS HENRY THOMPSON. I apprehended the prisoner on the 16th of Janurary - he had been out of the way ever since I took the other boys; he told me he could prove where he was when it wa stolen, that he was at Clerkenwell, with his mother or aunt.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280221-206]
686. RICHARD BOGNOR ROBINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of February, 1 pair of snuffers, value 1s. 6d., and 1 stand, value 1s., the goods of John George Gronett.
SUSANNAH GRONETT. I am the wife of John George Gronett - we keep a public-house in Spitalfields. The prisoner and two others were in the parlour in the afternoon of the 6th of February - I waited on them; they leftabout six o'clock - after being there two or three hours; they bid me good night, and one of them brought me the measure and glass - I was the first person who went into the parlour afterwards, and missed the snuffers and stand.
Prisoner. Q. Were we tipsey? A. I did not notice that - their reckoning did not exceed 4s.; the prisoner went out several times.
EBENEZER SMITH. I am a watchman. I was in Brick-lane on the 6th of February, about ten o'clock at night, and was called to search the prisoner on suspicion of another robbery - I found these snuffers and stand in his pocket; I saw him pulling off his coat as I went along the passage.
Prisoner. Q. Was not my coat some distance from me when you came in? A. It was off, but you were by the side of it; the landlord and another person were there, and a watch-maker; you had been drinking.
THOMAS HART. I am an officer, and received the prisoner in charge; he appeared in liquor, and said he knew nothing about it.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280221-207]
687. ALFRED WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of January, 1 pelisse, value 18s., the goods of Susan Williams, widow.
SUSAN WILLIAMS. I am a widow - the prisoner is my son; I go out to needle-work. On the 31st of January I went out at nine o'clock in the morning, returned in the evening, and missed my pelisse: the prisoner lived at home - he was never allowed to pawn my things.
BENJAMIN BARRETT. I am an officer, and took the prisoner; his mother told him, in my presence, that it was for taking her pelisse - and as we went to Hatton-garden, he said if it was not his own wish, his mother would not prosecute; but it was, or if she did not, very likely he should do something worse.
JOHN ABETHELL. I am a pawnbroker. The prisoner pawned this pelisse with me on the 31st of January.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 14.
Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18280221-208]
688. EDWARD TURNER was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of January, 2 quarts of brandy, value 12s., the goods of Joseph Bennett.
JOSEPH BENNETT. I keep the Bay Moulton public-house, in Gibson-street - the prisoner was my pot-boy - On the 29th of January I saw some wet on the floor in my cellar, and on the partition of the liquor cellar; I put my finger, and tasted it - it was brandy; I found part of the partition had been forced, and made to move up and down- it divides the liquor from the beer cellar; the prisoner had access to the beer cellar - I found a bottle of brandy in a copper; I kept a strict watch, but had occasion to go out; I returned in five minutes, went to the copper, and found nearly two quarts more of brandy, in a can; I called the street-keeper, and told him - we watched all night; by some means the prisoner laid very late that morning; I soon after received information from my brother - I went down, and caught the prisoner in the act of moving the bottle of brandy into the dust-hole; I saw him at the door of the dust-hole, and found the brandy in the hole - I said,
"I have caught you;" he made no reply, but told the Magistrate that he did not force the partition, but stole the brandy, and he thought the partition was broken by the brewers.
THOMAS BENNETT. I live with the prosecutor, and sat up with him in the morning. I was looking through the partition, and saw the prisoner moving the brandy from the copper; I was in the inner cellar; he crossed the kitchen, and took it to the dust-hole, where it was found.
JAMES MCINTOSH. I sat up with Bennett, and in the morning I was called, and took the prisoner; I heard him acknowledge to taking the brandy.
RICHARD HARTLEY WALL. I am an officer. The prisoner was brought to the office with the brandy.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18280221-209]
689. WILLIAM TRIGG was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of February, 1 tea-pot, value 2s., the goods of William Henry Royer.
WILLIAM HENRY ROYER. I am a broker, and live in Old-street. On the 11th of February I was coming home, and saw the prisoner draw his hand from my shop window; I ran up and missed this tea-pot - I ran and took him with it under his apron, walking away.(Property produced and sworn to.)
The prisoner pleaded distress.
GUILTY. Aged 40.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18280221-210]
690. FRANCES WRIGHT was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of February, 1 umbrella, value 5s., and 2 books, value 10s., the goods of William Parrington.
WILLIAM PARRINGTON. I keep the Horse and Groom public-house at Hackney ; the prisoner was my servant. On the 18th of January when I came down in the morning, my children gave me information; I missed a silk umbrella from its place, and the prisoner was out; she came back without it - I told her the children had seen her take it out - she denied it - I sent for an officer, who took her.
JOHN GODDARD. I am an officer, and was fatched. The prosecutor said his children had seen the prisoner take an umbrella out; she pointed to one which hung up, and said that was the one she took out, and had brought home again; The prosecutrix said that was there while she was out - I afterwards found this umbrella and two books at Bristow's.
JANE BRISTOW. The prisoner brought me an umbrella to repair on the 18th; and said she would call for it at twelve o'clock, as she had taken her place to go to Paddington; she asked if she might leave a small parcel, which she did, and it contained two books - I gave them all to the officer.
The prisoner put in a written defence, declaring that the articles were given her by Mrs. Perrington, instead of money, which she had agreed to allow her as chambermaid: but that the prosecutrix was frequently intoxicated and did not know what she was about; that the prosecutor had beaten his wife, and compelled her to deny giving them to her; it being done anknown to him, as they were constantly fighting.
JOHN GODDARD. She said nothing about the things being given to ber till her second examination at Worship-street - the case was remanded for Mrs. Perrington to confront her.
MRS. PERRINGTON. I did not give her the things; or allow her to take them or anything else.
Prisoner. She promised me some gowns and other things.
MRS.PERRINGTON. I did not; I had just bought the books for my little boy; I gave her nothing; when it was missed she said she never saw it - the top of the cap was broken; I had given her notice to leave that day.
GUILTY. Aged 42.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-211]
FOURTH DAY. MONDAY, FEBRUARY 25.
Fourth Middlesex Jury - Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
691. WILLIAM TOOMEY was indicted for stealing, on the 23d of October, 1 sack, value 1s., and 3 bushels of oats, value 8s., the goods of Thomas Young, and John Dudin Brown.
THOMAS YOUNG. I keep a granary; these oats were in the care of myself and John Dudin Brown, my partner; they were in a barge at King Henry-stairs - eight or nine men were employed to unload them.
EDWARD LONG. I am the prosecutors' foreman. I employed eight men to unload on the 23rd of October - the prisoner was not one of those men: but he brought a sack to the barge while I was on board; I knew him as a labourer about there; he had come to me in the morning, and said"Ned, you are a working very hard - I'll give you a spell," meaning to assist me. I said, "I thank you;" this was about ten o'clock, and about twelve he came and held a sack to me; I filled it - he carried it out of the barge; I expected he would take it into the granary, where the other men carried theirs - he took the sack from Haley, as it was his turn to bring a sack.
JAMES MULLENS. I was employed to unload the barge. The prisoner came there - he took sixteen or seventeen turns for another man; I saw him take a sack down a street, opposite King Henry-stairs; I do not know where to - I heard him tell Haley, he would take a sweat for him, as well as another man; he did not go towards the granary with it - I told Long of it; I was coming down the plank from the granary at the time.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. How long is it since you quarrelled with this man? A. I never quarrelled with him - we never exchanged blows. I was at Kingston three years ago - I was charged with robbery there.
Q. Had not the prisoner carried sixteen sacks of corn to the granary just before? A. No; about an hour before - he did not abscond.
JOHN DWYER. I was at work on board this barge, and saw the prisoner. I did not see him take the corn - I was in the granary, and missed a man out of his turn, as I received the corn from the men.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known the prisoner? A. Four or five years - he was well known, and has been about ever since, I believe.
MR.BODKIN to EDWARD LONG. Q. Does Haley live in the neighbourhood? A. I have see him several times. Mr. Young has not told me, he had sent to find him; I heard a Mr. Evans was looking after him one day, and could not find him; Hawkes was in the barge with the prisoner
Prisoner's Defence. I was employed to clear a shed of coals in Tooley-street; I went to Cane's wharf to get a barge to clear a ship, and met Hawkes who wanted a job; we saw these men at work - Long asked me to give him a spell, which I did, and then gave another man about twenty turns; a woman named Murphy sat on the stairs with apples; she spoke to me as I went in and out of the granary; at flood tide, I said I must go, as I was then to have a barge; I called a man and gave him up my sack - I stood talking to Murphy for ten minutes, and then went away to the harge.
- HAWKES. I was with the prisoner; he offered to give them a spell, and carried some sacks into the granary - I was with him when he left; we were both going to work in a coal-barge at flood-tide; when we left he had no sack or any thing of the kind; we left together about eleven o'clock, and I continued with him all day.
HANNAH MURPHY. I was selling apples on the 23d just by the stairs, and saw the prisoner carry corn into the granary twelve or fourteen times or more; I was there till after he went away - he took nothing away. I saw him carry the last sack up two planks, and then give it to a man; he then took his jacket and hat, and asked me to give him a pint of beer - he took an apple and went away.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280221-212]
692. ELIZABETH LEY was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of January, 1 handkerchief, value 1s.; 2 half-crowns, and 20 shillings the property of Thomas Dorren; and 8 shillings, and 1 sixpence, the monies of Thomas Waller.
MR.CHURCHILL conducted the prosecution.
THOMAS DORREN. I am a pauper of St. Martin's Work-house. On the 17th of January I had a box, with a handkerchief, four half-crowns, and some shillings - I found it broken open on the 19th, and the property taken.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do you know the prisoner? A. Yes; she belongs to the house, and has been deserted by her husband, she has three children.
ANN COLLINS. On the the 19th, the prisoner asked me to pawn a handkerchief, which I refused; I cannot swear to the handkerchief.
MARY LEY. The prisoner is my sister-in-law. On Thursday, the 20th of January, she left a handkerchief like this with me - I cannot say this is it; she was in great distress.
THOMAS DORREN. I cannot swear to the handkerchief; it is quite new.
JEREMIAH BEATON SMITH. I am master of the work-house. I found two sixpences, and 7 1/2d. on the prisoner, on hearing this complaint - I also found some keys, one of which opened the prosecutor's box.
Cross-examined. Q. Is it not a common key? A. I do not know.
THOMAS WALLER. I delivered to the prosecutor 8s.6d. to keep for me - one sixpence was marked with T.H., and a kind of wheel on it - this is it.
Cross-examined. Q. You did not make the mark? A. No; it was so when I took it.
Prisoner's Defence. I altered a gown for a woman in the house, who paid me that money.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280221-213]
693. JOHN MORRIS was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of January, 6 pieces of wood, value 4s., the goods of Thomas Mash, his master.
THOMAS MASH. The prisoner has been two years in my employ. I am a cabinet-maker, and live in Wardour-street; he has been alone in the shop lately. On the 29th of January, about eight o'clock, I came into the shop and saw him with this piece of wood in his hand, and asked what he was making, and said, "Are you authorized to cut up my wood?" He said "I have only taken this one piece." I went to the door, and when I came back he was gone; but in the top of his bench, I found these six pieces of wood; when he came in the evening, I said, "You have been robbing me." He said, "I meant to pay you for it." I never suffer my men to take wood and pay for it afterwards - I am pretty certain it is all mine - one piece I am sure of, and have the piece it was cut from; it is cut for a bedstead which I never set him to make - he has worked for me eight years.
BENJAMIN SCHOFIELD. I took the prisoner in charge; he said he had used some of his master's property; and mentioned two under stumps, and this under rail, and a head-board.
Prisoner's Defence. I had a job to repair a bedstead; master owed me a week's wages; I used this, intending to pay him.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280221-214]
604. JANE BURT was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of January, 2 sovereigns, 1 half crown, and 1 sixpence, the monies of Saul Josephs, from his person.
SAUL JOSEPHS. I am shopman to Mr. Phillips, a clothes salesman, of Shoreditch. On Sunday night, the 20th of January, about twenty minutes past eleven o'clock, I was in Kingsland-road, coming from the Canal-bridge towards Shoreditch-church - I was sober; I saw three women standing together - I passed them about three feet, when the prisoner followed me, and asked if I would go back a little way - I said, "Don't bother me, I have no time to stop;" I walked on, and she kept talking to me: when we got on about twenty-five yards she got right in front of me, and put her hand to the flap of my trowsers - I drew my right hand out of my trowsers pocket to keep her off; I had at that time my money in my pocket - there were two sovereigns, two half crowns, and one sixpence; I had not taken my hand out of my pocket two minutes before I felt her hand come from my pocket - I think it was her left hand; I immediately felt, and found only a half crown left - I took her by the wrist, and said she had robbed me, and if she did not return my money I would give her in charge; she said she wanted to stop a minute, and turned up a street - I followed her, and kept hold of her hand till she stooped down - I stood as close to her as I thought decent, to watch if she threw the money away, as I thought it was only a pretence - she was stooping down for about a minute; when she got up, I said if she did not return my money I would give her in charge - I caught hold of her right hand; Mr. Mantz was passing - I asked him for assistance; he caught hold of her hand, and told her she had better give me my money - I called the watchman; she then gave me some money, saying,"Here is 18d. for you;" when I got it in my hand, I said it was a sovereign and a sixpence - I was then holding one hand, and Mr. Mantz the other; I had told Mantz I had lost two sovereigns and some silver before she gave me the 18d.; two watchmen came up - I then had the sovereign and sixpence in my hand - I held it to the light to shew that it was a sovereign and a sixpence; I desired a watchman to go behind her with a light, as we went to the watch-house - on the road she said she had dropped a halfpenny - somebody picked it up, and said it was half a crown - I had heard something drop.
Cross-examined by MR.PHILLIPS. Q. Does your master keep a shop? A. Yes - we do not buy any thing- I attend the shop; no sovereign was found on the prisoner; I did not feel her put her hand into my pocket - it is a large one, and has no button; I was standing at the time; I had been part of the way to Dalston with a gentleman - I had been at the Eagle, in the City-road, and had had three glasses of gin and water between three of us; I swear I gave the prisoner no money whatever, and had no intention of going with her; I told Bevan, the watchman, I had lost a sovereign and some silver; as one sovereign had then been returned.
Q. After she was searched, did not you say she robbed you of one sovereign and 6s.? A. I did not - I did not ask her for 2l. at the office.
WILLIAM CHRISTOPHER MANTZ. I am a printer, and live in Brick-lane, Spitalfields. I was coming along Kingsland-road, and by a street opposite some alms-houses I saw the prosecutor and prisoner in altercation; the prosecutor said,
"If you are a gentleman I hope you will stop and act as such - this woman has robbed me;" I asked what he was robbed of - he mentioned a sovereign and some silver pieces, but what I do not recollect; she said she had not robbed him, and had only 2s., which he had given her; I said, "Has he been with you?" and she said, No; I said, "Then give him the 2s., and save yourself from the watch-house" - she then began to struggle, as if inclined to fight - I took hold of her left hand, which was clenched tight - she directly opened it, and gave the prosecutor two pieces of money; I do not think she said what they were - the watchman then came up and as they took her along the prosecutor said to me, "She thinks she has given me 2s., but, by the weight of it, one is a sovereign;" he opened his hand at the lamp, and there was a sovereign and a sixpence; when we were in Thomas-street, the prisoner said, "I have dropped a halfpenny" - I saw something fall, and heard it - I picked it up, and it was half a crown; I kept it till we got to the watch-house, and there marked it.
Cross-examined. Q. When she said she had dropped a halfpenny, had any one hold of her hands? A. I think not - there was a watchman on each side of her; the prosecutor walked very close to her - she gave the first intimation of it; I was very near her, and cannot say whether anything might fall from her without my seeing it; I did not notice any body behind her with a light.
Q. When she gave the prosecutor the money, did he say one was a 6d.? A. Not that I recollect, but I did not take much notice; she did not say, "Here is 18d."
EVAN JONES. I am a watchman. I heard a call of Watch! and went up; I saw the prisoner held by the prosecutor and Mantz; he said she had robbed him of two sovereigns and some silver; he could not tell how much -she denied it; she had 2s., which she said he had given her to go with him, but she did not like to do so: he said,"Here is a sovereign and 6d., which she calls 18d." As we took her to the watch-house, she dropped something from her side, in Thomas-street; I said, "What is that?" she said "Only a half-penny" - it was picked up, and was a half-crown.
Cross-examined. Q. He could not tell how much silver he had lost? A. Not exactly; they were about twelve yards up a new street, and running up to the road: I saw them, before Mr. Mantz came up, walking together; and, in about five minutes there was a quarrel - I did not see her stoop.
COURT. Q. Did you not tell the Magistrate you saw them standing and taking before you went up? A. I did.
WILLIAM BEVAN. I was constable of the night. The prisoner was brought in and charged by the prosecutor with taking his money; I asked him what, he said 47s. or 48s. - that there were two sovereigns, a half-crown, and the rest in silver. I had joined them in the street, and think the half-crown fell from under her petticoats in Thomas-street; she said."Never mind, it is only a halfpenny;" I said, "Let us look," and it was a half-crown.
Cross-examined. Q. Were not some halfpence found on her? A. I heard so; I think she said it was only a halfpenny, before any one said she had dropped something; but I cannot say whether she or Mantz spoke first.
JOHN WILLIAM WINTLE. I searched the prisoner at the watch-house, and found four shillings, three pence, and two halfpence upon her, but no sovereigns - the prosecutor was rather fresh; he said she had robbed him of two sovereigns and some odd silver, and had given him back, as she said, eighteen shillings.
Cross-examined. Q. What did he say he had lost next morning? A. A sovereign, half-a-crown, and a shilling or sixpence - I believe he said at night it was 47s. or 48s., but she had given him back a sovereign and half; but he could not tell till the morning what silver he had lost - he appeared to know what he was about, but rather fresh.
COURT. Q. Had he not his faculties about him? A. Yes; he was confused for a moment.
Prisoner. I leave myself to the mercy of the Court.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18280221-215]
Fifth Middlesex Jury - before Mr. Common Sergeant.
695. JOSEPH HATCHETT was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of January, 5 live tame geese, price 20s.; 7 live tame ducks, price 10s., and 8 live tame fowls, price 8s., the property of Charles Broad.
MR. QUIN conducted the prosecution.
JAMES HARVEY. I am servant to Charles Broad, of Hornsey. On the 28th of January all his poultry was safe, and on the next morning I missed them; the place was broken open, the dog loosened, and put into an out-house, and fastened in - the prisoner worked for Mr. Broad at times - I gave an alarm and found the poultry at the watch-house; five ducks had escaped and were swimming about.
JAMES DAVIS. I am a watchman of Hornsey. On Monday night, the 28th of January, I had just called half-past eleven o'clock, and met Hall; I had seen the prisoner; he passed us, and said,
"There you are watchman; "I said Yes - he had not got far before a patrol gave an alarm that some men had run from a field; we went towards the field; the prisoner passed us again; I knew him; he was going towards home - we went on to the field, and there I found two geese, two ducks, and a fowl in a ditch - they were dead; the fowl's head was cut off; the geese and ducks necks were broken; they were quite warm - the clock had not struck twelve when we found them.
ABRAHAM HALL. I was with Davis and found the poultry; we took it to the watch-house.
JOHN MORGAN. I am an officer. I received information, and apprehended the prisoner on the 29th of January; I had been to Mr. Broad's premises and saw some footsteps, which I compared with the prisoner's shoes; they fitted exactly, in size, the number of nails, and the size of the heads of the nails - I traced them from the hen-house to a moat, and then out of the premises into a field, which leads to the road: the prisoner's breeches had a great deal of blood on them, which appeared quite fresh, and some mud was put over it to hide it - a large piece was taken out of the front of his breeches, and only the lining left. He at first said the blood came from his nose bleeding, and afterwards from kiling a pig.
EDWARD JOHNSON. I am a headborough, and live behind the Coach and Horses public-house, at Holloway. The prisoner's mother has a room at my house, and since he has been out of work, she has let him lodge there, as he could not pay his lodgings. On the night of the 28th of January, I heard him come home; I was in bed: I awoke and heard the watchmen call twelve o'clock directly after I heard him come in - I was sure it was him; he came to the window and called mother twice; his mother or sister went down softly and let him in; I heard him go up stairs rather heavily; I thought there were two or three people, and next morning early, Mr. Broad's servant came in a chaise and said they had lost the fowls - I took the prisoner's shoes and compared them with the marks; they corresponded exactly: it is a new made orchard, which is very clayey - we traced them mark for mark, and nail for nail; I saw the prisoner at the watch-house, and noticed his breeches, with a piece cut out in front, and more spots of blood below.
THOMAS GRAFTON. I am a constable. The prisoner was brought to the watch-house: I assisted in comparing the shoes; they corresponded exactly with the marks - here are the shoes; I particularly observed this place; there is a nail out. Three geese, five fowls, and the rest of the duckes were found in a bag on the premises - the prisoner said at first, that the blood on his breeches was caused by fighting; and afterwards that he had been killing a pig. There must have been a good deal of blood on the person who did it, as the heads had been pulled off, and cut off.
JOHN PARTRIDGE. I am a headborough. I assisted in taking the prisoner, and, as I was taking him to prison, he said "I have been drawn into this innocently, as I was into the other." I had not asked him any questions.
Prisoner's Defence. I was at Mr. Gregson's, Hornsey-road, at the time of the robbery; I had to do a tree whichhad fallen down; the spade caught my breeches and tore them down: after work, I went to a public-house, and left it about ten o'clock at night; when I came out, a woman asked me if there was a stage to town; I said No, but I would see her to Holloway; when we got there, she said she was going to Bishopsgate-street, and, it being late, I walked with her; she satisfied me for my trouble.
ELIZABETH WRIGHT. I live at Johnson's, where the prisoner's mother lodges; I am sure he was at home before the clock struck twelve on this night; I do not know how long before; I did not see him, but heard his sister let him in - I had been asleep: I went to bed about ten o'clock; my little dog made a noise when he came in; the prisoner made a noise, and I thought seemed tipsy - the clock struck twelve, when he was in his room.
EDWARD JOHNSON. My house is about half a mile from Hornsey.
Prisoner. They might take my shoes and make the marks with them - the officer has a spite against me.
EDWARD JOHNSON. I did no such thing; we took particular notice of the marks, before we put the shoes down; there were other footsteps smaller, and without nails.
JOHN MORGAN. The breeches appeared to be cut, and not torn down, it is a square place.
GUILTY. Aged 25.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-216]
696. JAMES GREEN was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of January, 1 handkerchief, value 2s., the goods of Louis Caillier, from his person.
LOUIS CAILLIER. On the 29th of January I was near Charing-cross, looking at two boys fighting; a boy told me my handkerchief was gone, and pointed out the prisoner; I went after him - he was stopped directly, just by Charing-cross; a very few yards off, and I took my handkerchief from inside his clothes, between his legs - my handkerchief was safe ten minutes before.
GEORGE JAMES JENNINGS. I am an officer. I found the prisoner in custody, and took him - the handkerchief was between his legs.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. A boy ran round the crowd; I picked the handkerchief up.
GUILTY. Aged 17.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-217]
697. WILLIAM HOWSON was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of January, 1 load of dung, value 2s. 6d., the goods of James Pope.
JOSEPH GIBSON. I am foreman to James Pope, of Edmonton. On the 31st of January a person said a man had taken a load of dung from the side of the dung-heap; I went to the prisoner's master, who sent it back that night; he lives two miles off.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Is not dung difficult to identify? A. Yes; I found it in the prisoner's cart, three or four hundred yards off - the cart was standing still at the time; I do not know which way he was going. I know his master would take him back; I believe he was led into it.
ROBERT BURT. I work for Mr. Pope. On the evening of the 31st of January I saw the prisoner at the end of the dung-heap, loading dung into the cart; he took about half the small load; there was another man with him - I went and said, "What are you doing?" they said, "We have put a load down, and are taking it up again;" I said,"I dare say you are;" they went on, and I told the prisoner's master of it. I spoke to the watchman, and went after the cart towards London.
Cross-examined. Q. Which of them were loading? A. The prisoner and the other.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Recommended to Mercy. - Fined 1s. and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18280221-218]
698. THOMAS HOLMES was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of February, 1 pair of boots, value 2s.6d., the goods of Thomas Horsley, from the person of Matthew Horsley .
THOMAS HORSLEY. I am a labourer. My son Matthew and his sister went to school on the 8th of February, a little after twelve o'clock; his sister came home without him - a man brought him home between one and two o'clock.
GEORGE HULL. I am a carpenter. On the 8th of February, about noon, I was coming out of my house, and heard a man had taken a pair of shoes from a child; I went in pursuit, and came up to the prisoner - I asked if he had not taken a pair of shoes from a child; he said No, that he had just come from Bow, and had seen none; I said he could not come from Bow that way. I took his hat off, and there found the shoes - I gave him to an officer.
JOHN MARSH CATT. I am an officer. I came up, and took the prisoner; in about five minutes the mother brought the child into the public-house; there were a dozen people there - I asked the child to point out the person who took the shoes; he pointed to the prisoner immediately, and said,
"That is the man, but he did not give me the penny"- the prisoner said he found the shoes on a bank.
The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that he had found the shoes in a field, and put them into his hat; and had not seen the child.
GUILTY. Aged 61
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18280221-219]
699. SOPHIA LOW was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of February, 2 half-sovereigns, 10 shillings, and 5 sixpences, the monies of John Ashdown, from his person.
JOHN ASHDOWN. On the 13th of February, about nine o'clock in the evening, I met the prisoner in Whitechapel; she asked me to go home with her - I refused; she called me aside, and said she wanted to speak to me, and from curiosity I went up a passage with her, to hear what she would say; she drew a handful of money out of my pocket - I said,
"You have robbed me;" the patrol came up, and she threw it on the ground; I had about 4l. 10s. - it was three half-sovereigns, one sovereign, and some silver; it was all in my right-hand breeches pocket.
Cross-examined by MR.PHILLIPS. Q. Are you married? A. Yes, and have a family; I had been in Marklane all day, and had been in a public-house on business, in Mark-lane, and then to another in Crutched-friars, and had some half-and-half with some friends - I did not spend 2s. altogether; I was not quite sober; I cannot say whether I had any gin and water, or rum and water: I went down a dark passage, but for no improper purpose; I did not give her any thing. I live in Harvey-terrace, Commercial-road; she did not say I had given her 2s.
WILLIAM MITCHELL. I am an officer. On the night of the 13th of February I heard a cry of Watch! up Barleymow-court; I ran up; Ashdown had hold of the prisoner, and said, "She has robbed me;" I took hold of her hand, and said, "Have you robbed him?" she said No; I held her, sent for a light, and found a half sovereign, three half-crowns, a shilling, and six sixpences; the prosecutor said next morning that he lost 1l. 12s. 6d. I took her to the watch-house, and 18d. was found on her; she said he had offered her 5s.
Cross-examined. Q. In what state was the prosecutor? A. He was not sober; he gave us some beer after we had locked her up - he said that night that he had lost a considerable deal of money; he seemed too drunk to tell how much he had lost - he said he could not tell till he looked at his books - they were about ten yards down the passage.
CHARLES COOK. I was with Mitchell, and found the money.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he say what he had lost? A. He said he could not tell till he looked at his books at home.
Prisoner's Defence. I heard some money fall, as he took it from his pocket; he asked me for 2s. back which he had given me; I refused; he seized me, and they fell from my hand.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280221-220]
700. HENRY MILLER was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of February, 2 handkerchiefs, value 2s., the goods of George Borough, from his person.
GEORGE BOROUGH. On the 10th of February I was at the Castle public-house, Uxbridge, leaning over the bench, speaking to a person, and found the prisoner's hand in my pocket; I took hold of it, and said, "You are taking my handkerchiefs - you had better let that alone; "I sat down, and said,
"I will have them again;" I gave him in charge as he was abusive; I saw them again next morning; one of them was torn to pieces.
Prisoner. Q. What were you doing? A. Speaking to a book-hawker - I was not taking his watch out.
JOHN FARRANT. I am a constable. I was sent for, and took the prisoner; the prosecutor gave me the handkerchiefs next morning.
GEORGE BOROUGH re-examined. They were brought to me next morning by a young fellow, who had been to see the prisoner in the cage; I heard the prisoner examined, and saw him sign this account - (read.)
"The prisoner admits he took the handkerchiefs, but it was only out of a lark."
Prisoner's Defence. We had been drinking together all day; he was leaning over, and taking a man's watch - I took his hankerchiefs, and sent them to him next morning.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280221-221]
701. SAMUEL PAYNE was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of December, 80 lbs. weight of lead, value 12s., belonging to Thomas Lynn, and fixed to a certain building of his; against the Statute.
THOMAS LYNN. I am a builder, and own a house in Brunswick-street, Hackney-road, in the parish of Shoreditch. On Thursday morning, the 20th of December, I missed some lead from the gutters, which I had seen safe two days before; part of it was afterwards produced - I could not match it, as the rest was gone; I know it by my own dressing, as I am my own plumber - here are marks which I put on it about six months ago, when I put it down on the gutter of Nos. 5 and 7, in the street - I lost lead from other gutters before, but it was older than this - I am certain this belongs to No. 5 or 7, and I saw it safe two days before; the mark on the lead of the other houses is quite different.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is this the lead of a whole gutter? A. No, only part of it - it has been cut in pieces; I marked the other in a different place.
ELIJAH BEASLEY. I live at the Roebuck public-house, Hackney-road. On the 20th of December Pool called to me and my fellow-servant to stop the prisoner; we found this lead in his possession; I said,
"You have been and stolen this lead from Mr. Lynn's houses;" he said, "You are a poor fellow as well as myself - you had better let me go."
GEORGE HARDING. I and Beasly were called, and took the prisoner with the lead.
EGGETT POOL. I was selling fruit at the corner of Haggerston-lane - the back of these houses come down to this lane. On the 20th of December, Mr.Lynn asked if I had seen any body with lead - I said No; but about half past six o'clock, I saw a young man loaded with lead - I ran over to the public-house; but he had got down a back street; I should know him by his clothes - In about an hour, I saw the prisoner come with some lead - I ran to the public-house, and the witnesses came and took him.
Cross-examined. Q. How long is it since you were a witness here? A. I never was a witness; I was here, but did not do the misdemeanor; I was brought here about some beef, and imprisoned six months - I was tipsy.
JAMES BROWN. I am an officer, and received the lead from the witnesses, and I told the witnesses if they saw the prisoner to take him, and a night or two after, I went with them and took him - I went to the houses, and could not match the lead except by the width of the gutters which seemed to match.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not you see footsteps on the ground? A. Yes; I took the prisoner's shoes off, but did not match them, as I was informed he had got a new pair; the marks were of a shoe with part of the heel worn off.
ELIJAH BEASLEY. I left the prisoner with Harding while I went for assistance, and he escaped - I had seen him before about.
Cross-examined. Q. Did the prosecutor attempt to swear to the lead before he spoke to you? A. He spoke to me before he went in; the Magistrate did not reprimand him in my hearing.
GEORGE HARDING re-examined. I knew the prisoner before - he struggled and got from me - some persons round said, if I did not let him go they would make me.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not the prosecutor speak to you? A. He did, outside the office; he told me to be confident to the lead, and not swear to the wrong lead, as there was other lead about; the magistrate reprimanded him for speaking to me - Beasley was present, but he had sworn to it before he spoke to me - I told the magistrate I had seen the prisoner before.
The witnesses deposition being read, did not contain that statement.
I told the magistrate so I am certain.
Prisoner's Defence. Mr. Lynn said he could not swear to it, before he called these lads out; he then came in, and said he could swear to it - the Magistrate reprimanded him for it.
GUILTY Aged 19.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-222]
702. WILLIAM JONES and JOHN BURNS were indicted for stealing, on the 19th of January, 1 handkerchief, value 2s., the goods of John Short Hewitt, Doctor of Divinity, from his person.
REV. JOHN SHORT HEWITT, D. D. On the 19th of January, I was in Greek-street; a man touched me on the shoulder, and said I had been robbed - I felt, and my handkerchief was gone - I described it, and gave my card, and in about three quarters of an hour, a man came to my hotel, and said the men were in custody - I found the prisoners at Marlborough-street office with it.
ANGELIOUS BETRAUN. I am an officer. About eleven o'clock, on the 19th of January, I watched the prisoners from Soho-square to Greek-street. I there saw Burns take a handkerchief from Dr. Hewitt's pocket, and run up Rose-street - I went and got the prosecutor's card; then went and took Jones at the corner of Rose-street; I waited there till Boston came back with Burns; they were close together when the handkerchief was taken - I knew Jones before; but never heard any thing against him.
JOHN BOSTON. I was with Betraun, and saw Morris take the handkerchief; I pursued, and took it from his coat pocket.(Property produced and sworn to.)
BURN'S Defence. I saw nothing of Jones, except at the watch-house.
JONES' Defence. I know nothing of the other prisoner.
BURNS - GUILTY. Aged 18.

703. JOHN WILKIE and GEORGE AYLESWORTH were indicted for stealing, on the 17th of January, 1 piece of silk handkerchief, value 20s. the goods of Charles Barker.
WILLIAM BARKER. I live in Edgeware-road, and am a haberdasher. On the evening of the 17th of January, about 7 o'clock, two boys alarmed me - I missed a piece of handkerchiefs off a bar, four feet inside the shop; they were safe five minutes before: Charles Barker is owner of the shop - I had been into the kitchen.
Prisoner AYLESWORTH. Q. Did you not say at the office, that you took Wilkie into your shop? A. I went out and brought a lad in, who I thought was Wilkie; and while I fetched a boy to recognize him he was gone.
EDMOND IMBER. I know the prisoners by seeing them several times about the Edgeware-road; on the evening of Thursday, on the 17th of January, I saw Wilkie come up to Mr. Barker's door, and go in on his hands and knees - Aylesworth was standing at the window; Wilkie snatched down a length of something - I could not see what; he came out and they ran away - I alarmed the people in the shop, and saw the prisoners at the office on the Saturday.
HENRY LANGLEY. I know the prisoners. On the 17th of January, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening, I saw them standing at Barker's door - Aylesworth stood at the window; Wilkie went into the shop on his hands and knees, and pulled down a long length of something - they ran away; I am certain they are the boys.
PHILIP WEBSTER. I received a description, and took the prisoners on the 19th, but found no goods on them.
WILKIE - GUILTY. Aged 17.

704. SARAH WOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of February, 1 jacket, value 10s., the goods of John Blackburn.
JOHN BLACKBURN. I am a sailor. On the 20th of February, about ten o'clock at night, I met the prisoner in the street, and went with her to a house; and while I was asleep I was disturbed by my trousers falling, which I had put at the bed-side - I got up immediately, and said I believed I was robbed - I felt my trousers'-pocket, and missed two sovereigns, and 7l. in silver; the prisoner was not then in the room; the watchman came in, and called me - he told me to dress; I got up, and could not find my jacket - I am sure the prisoner is the woman I went there with - she had not come to bed.
THOMAS HOBBS. I am a watchman. On the evening of the 20th of February I was in Blue Anchor-yard, and saw the prisoner and the prosecutor go into Harebrain-court; I met the prisoner some time afterwards in Newmarket-street, with this jacket under her apron; I asked what she had got; she said it was nothing to me - I took her, and found it was this jacket - I left her in charge, and got the prosecutor up.
JOHN DUNGALE. The prisoner was brought to the watch-house with the jacket.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. He left the jacket with me instead of money, as he lost his money; I said I would leave it where it would be safe.
GUILTY. Aged 34.

700. JOSEPH MADDOCKS was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of January, 1 plane, value 1s. 6d., the goods of Joseph Owen; and 1 plane, value 1s. 6d., the goods of Robert Cuthbert.
JOSEPH OWEN. I am a carpenter. I lost this plane out of the yard at the City-bason; my house is close by there, in Messrs. Snell and Co's. yard; it was in the yard, I believe; I saw it at the Carpenter's Arms public-house - it had G.O. upon it; the prisoner did not work in the yard; I knew him in the country, and he used to come and see me.
ROBERT CUTHBERT. I work in the same yard, and lost a plane on the 16th of January; when I came to work, in the morning, it was gone - I had used it on the Saturday before; the shop is locked up at night; when I left I found out the man that had bought the plane, and then the prisoner was taken; he had no business to be in the yard.
- HOWELL. I bought two planes of the prisoner, on Wednesday the 11th of January, between ten and eleven o'clock at night, at a public-house, in Old-street; we both lodged there; I knew he had no employment; I gave 1s. for the two; he was in much distress.
JOHN CLARK. I produce the planes Howell gave me. I found the prisoner at the Merry Carpenters public-house - and he went with me to the watch-house.(Property produced and sworn to.)

707. ALEXANDER MACDONALD was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of December, 1 saddle, value 15s., the goods of Thomas Sell.
JEREMIAH AISH. I lived with Mr. Thomas Sell, of Chiswell-street, in October last, when the saddle was lost; the prisoner worked occasionally there; the saddle was in one of the stables - I cannot say when I saw it last; Asbury came to me with the duplicate - it was pawned in Fore-street; when I saw it, I was not quite sure that it was my master's; Asbury asked me to buy the ticket - the prisoner never worked in the yard.
JAMES ASBURY. I am a sadler, and live at No. 7, White-street, Little Moor-fields. The prisoner called upon me with the duplicate of a saddle, on or about the 12th of January; he wished me to buy it - I thought he was an honest man - it was pawned for 9s. or 7s. - I would have nothing to do with it; he said the duplicate belonged to another man; I told him if it was worth buying, I knew a person who would buy it - I went to Jeremiah Aish, and we went to Fore-street and took it out, and looked at it; he asked me what the person wanted for it, I told him 9s., he said he would give 5s., and something for himself; the prisoner was taken up that day.
JOB VALENTINE WATKINS. I am shopman to Jacob Russel, a pawnbroker, No. 43. Fore-street. The saddle was redeemed by Jeremiah Aish; it was pawned on the 25th of December, by the prisoner, in his own name; I asked if it was his brother Harry's; he said it was; I advanced 7s. upon it - it is worth about 14s.; it was an old one; it was looked at first - about two hours after Aish came and took it out.
JOHN BROWN. I am a constable. The prisoner was given into my custody, with the saddle.
JEREMIAH AISH re-examined. I have no doubt at all but that the saddle is my master's; the prisoner worked in the yard when his brother sent him - his brother used to come into the yard.
Prisoner's Defence. A man came and asked for Macdonald - he said he had a saddle to sell; I told him I did not think he wanted one - he asked me to buy it - I said I had not money enough, and he asked me if I would pawn it; I asked him where he got it - he said from a man at the White Bear public-house; I went to pawn it - I told him to put my name on the ticket - I bought the ticket, and told other people so.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Ten Days.

708. JAMES McCARTHY was indicted for stealing, on the 23d of January, 1 handkerchief, value 4s., the goods of Thomas Nicholls.
SAMUEL HAMPSTEAD. I am in the employ of Thomas Nicholls, a pawnbroker, of Gray's Inn-lane. This handkerchief was pinned inside the shop door, with about half a dozen others; a person must have put his arm inside the door, but he need not step into the shop; I had seen the prisoner there about five minutes before - I did not see him take it; Bennett pointed out a man to me - I jumped over the counter; I went out after him, but could not see the prisoner till he was at Hatton-garden, about half an hour afterwards; there was a ticket on it, of the price for sale; that had been taken off it.
FRANCIS BENNETT. I am a labourer. About a quarter or twenty minutes before one o'clock, I saw the prisoner put his hand into the shop - he took a handkerchief, and put it into the flap of his trousers; I gave information of it at the shop directly; I pursued, and never lost sight of him; I am quite sure I took the same man, and took him to Hatton-garden. When I collared him he threw the handkerchief down - the officer picked it up.
JOSEPH CADBY. I had just come out of the office in Hatton-garden; Bennett had hold of the prisoner, I saw him throw the handkerchief from him, and I picked it up.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I deny all knowledge of the robbery; I never saw the handkerchief before.
GUILTY. Aged 44.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280221-231]
709. THOMAS RIORDAN was indicted for that he, on the 9th of October, in the fortieth year of the reign of George the Third, at St. Peter, in the City of Cork, in that part of Great Britian, called Ireland, was married to Catherine Roberts, spinster, and her had for his wife, and afterwards, and during the life of his said wife, on the 17th of May, in the forty-second year of the reign of the said King George the Third, at St. Paul, Covent-garden, in the name of Thomas Reading, feloniously did marry Ann Barton, and her did take as his wife; against the Statute, &c.
MR. BODKIN conducted the prosecution
CHARLES SHEEN. I produce a certificate, which is from the church of St. Peter's, at Cork; I received it from the hands of John Forsyth, the minister; I compared the names and dates with the register - it is a true copy; that enables me to swear that the names and dates are the same.
Cross-examined by MR. P. ALLEY. Q. Where do you live? A. At Greenwich. I went from England to Cork to get the copy of the register; I did not know any one at Cork, nor the minister; I went to St. Peter's, and got this from the parson at the church, and then I brought it here; I swear I took the book, and compared the hand-writing - the signature, and that also: the parson was a very old man; I mean to say I had my hand on the book while I read it - the parson did not read it to me.
The certificate was read, the purport of which was that Thomas Riorden was married to Catherine Roberts on the 9th of October, 1799, at St. Peter's Church, Cork, according to the ceremonies of the Established Church of the United Kingdom.
MR. ADOLPHUS objected that there was a variation in the the date of the marriage according to the certificate, and the year of the King's reign set out in the indictment. And that they could not have been married according to the rights of the Church of the "United Kingdom" for, according to the certificate, it was before the union took place.
ELLEN LE GRESSE. My name before marriage was Ellen Roberts. In the year 1799, the latter part of it, I was living in Cork; I was there, I believe, on the 9th of October - I had a sister named Catherine; I was married then. I know the prisoner - he was then living at Cork, as a carpenter; my sister was married twenty-nine years ago - it was in the month stated; I remember the day of the month - I went with them to the church door, and then returned to help my mother to prepare the wedding dinner - they returned in about three quarters of an hour; my sister went by the name of Catherine Roberts, and after marriage by the name of Catherine Riordan; they lived in my father's house, as man and wife; I heard Riordan say after the marriage, that Mr. Forsyth married them; they lived at my father's for four months, as man and wife; they then lived a short distance off: my father and mother are both dead. In about four months he left my sister in a state of pregnancy, without any notice. Five years ago I came over to England, and saw the prisoner; I had never before seen him since he left - an arrangement was come to; he said he would settle a house on my sister Catherine, and told me he would come to Greenwich on the Sunday following, to settle; he came on the Saturday evening, but so drunk that no business could be done. - About a month after that I met him on Blackfriars-bridge - he gave me 1l., and said that he would allow my sister 2s. 6d. per week - he continued to do so for about two years and a half, and I sent it to my sister at Cork. During this transaction he sometimes called my sister Kitty, and sometimes his wife. At the end of two years and a half, he said he could not live longer with his second wife, and if I would send for her over, he would either take her home, or set her up in business; he gave me 4l. to send over to bring her over; he had three children by his second wife: my sister is still living - my sister did not then come over; she came over last June or July; she was ill at the time the money was given - that was about two years(when she came over) after the money was given. The child by the first wife is dead. When my sister came over my husband wrote, and the prisoner came down to Greenwich he saw her, and said he would put her into business, but she objected to a school, as being too sedentary for her- he slept at my house that night; he said he was living in Exmouth-street, but he did not come down again. I next saw him in Exmouth-street; he was living with a young woman there, and had two children.
Cross-examined. Q. This was in 1799? A. Yes; I do not know that he was apprenticed to his father; my sister is about fifty-six years old - she was then about twenty-seven: I had lived in Cork all my life - I never saw his father; I have heard since that he was an apprentice to his father: I did not then know that he was more than seventeen - I know he was then more than twenty. I remained in Cork all my life; until about five years ago- my sister was Protestant; he was a Catholic - I had heard him say so - I left her in Ireland.
Q. Did she not keep a house herself before her marriage? A. She did; in Cold-lane - she kept it about six years; she was not keeping that house when she was married - she was living with my father. I have lately been on good terms with the prisoner; my husband did borrow a little money of him, and he paid it - it was lent before this charge was made, and it was paid before; I swear that my husband is not in debt to him - he would do nothing for her; I did not know the second wife until lately - I heard of it five years ago; we did not wish to make any charge if he would have provided for my sister.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Have you brought the charge against him at all? A. No. I only know that he is a Catholic by what he said.
JAMES HILL. I am clerk to Messrs, Selby, Bowden and Co., vestry-clerks of Clerkenwell. I produce an extract of the register of St. Paul Covent-garden; I received it from the Rev. Mr. Vickery; he is the curate of that parish; I received it in the vestry of that parish, and compared it with the register - it is a true copy.
This cerificate was read, and stated, that Thomas Reading was married to Ann Barton, on the 17th of May, 1801, by Edward Embrey, curate.
MR. ADOLPHUS took an objection to this certificate, on the same ground as the former one: there was a variance in the date of the certificate, and the year stated in the indictment.
ANN BARTON. I live at No. 6, Bedford-court, New North-street, Red Lion-square. I became acquainted with the prisoner in 1800; I never knew he was married till about three months back; I was married to him in 1801 - I was never before married - I lived with him twenty-four years and two months; I have had as good as twenty children by that marriage - there were born alive six sons and three daughters; we separated three years ago, next July: I was obliged to leave him for his ill usage.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. How old are the children now living? A. One seventeen, another fifteen and the girl eight years; the boy who is seventeen is bound to his father - the other is bound to Mr. Brown of Exmouth-street, tailor; the girl her father took care of - the children were taken care of at both our expence; one he apprenticed to himself - I cannot say who paid the apprentice fee with the other. When I left him, I did not take about 200l. in cash - he is very false if he says so; I only took my clothes and a few books - he made me an allowance for about two years, but it was not paid - he was about twenty-two years old when he married me.
Prisoner's Defence. I have got a witness who can prove that I was an apprentice to my father, and never was away from home till I came to England.
ELIZABETH PRENDERGAST. I am the prisoner's sister, I was living with my father when my brother was said to he married; my brother lived with him also; when they were married he was apprentice to my father - he was about eighteen years of age; my father never gave his consent - indeed neither my father nor mother knew anything about it - he did not sleep one night out of my father's house before the date of the certificate (the certificate of the first marriage.)
MR. BODKIN. Q. Did you know of the marriage? A. No: I read in the newspapers the date of the certificate; it was in 1799 - he slept at my father and mother's house three months after he was said to be married.
COURT. Q. When was he born? A. I believe in 1781. I was born in 1778; I did not know Robert's family - she lived in Cold-lane herself; I did not know her father and mother: I have heard my brother speak of her, but I never heard him say that he was married to her: it was reported, but there was no certainty of it - my father never asked him about it: he sent her away that he might not have anything more to do with her.
Mr. P. ALLEY submitted that there was an end to the case, the witnesses on the other side had only made the prisoner twenty years old. - Mr. BODKIN suggested that the 26 Geo. II. did not extend to Ireland.
The Jury found the prisoner GUILTY,
But that he was under age at the time of the first marriage, and that it was solemnized without his parents' consent. This case is reserved for the opinion of the Twelve Judges.
[trialAccount t18280221-232]
710. JOHN ROAKE was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of November, 1 bushel of beans, value 6s., the goods of George Winch.
MR. BODKIN conducted the prosecution.
JAMES HYDE. I and the prisoner were in the employ of George Winch, who is a corn-dealer, at Shepperton. I carried no beans for the prisoner in November; I carried malt and barley to his mother's.
HENRY GOLDHAWK. I was, on the 10th of November last, in the employ of Mr. Winch; the prisoner was so also at that time; I was desired by him to carry about a bushel of beans about the 10th; I was to carry them to his lodgings, at his mother's house; I did not see them put into the sack - he gave me them out of my master's granary; he said he wanted me to take a bushel of beans; he went with me - when we got there, he took them out of my hand, in a shed on his mother's premises - it was about eight o'clock in the morning.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. It was at the time when farmers are in full business? A. It was about eight o'clock in the morning; he met me at his mother's; the prosecutor's house is opposite to the farm, and any body could see what he was taking along; Mrs. Roake's house is about a quarter of a mile off; he never said such a thing as, "Don't let my master know it;" I did not know that my master had dealings with Mrs. Roake; he had his victuals at his mother's, but I believe he mostly slept at Weybridge; the prisoner remained with his master till February; he passed by me on my way to his mother's.
GEORGE WINCH. I am a corn-factor, at Shepperton. The prisoner was authorized by me to sell at all times; he was to make every entry on the day it happened - my book is here; I have examined it - I find no entry of beans sold to his mother on the 10th of November, nor to any other person - not on his own account, nor his mother's.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. The prisoner is executor under his late father's will? A. I believe so; I am not under any obligations to him - I am not aware that he had lent me money - if it was so, it was his own fault, for not making up his accounts; many times there were monies received, but he never lent me 50l. - I have had money when I have applied for it and he has said, "That is all I have got;" the balance has been sometimes in my favour, and sometimes in his; I remember his going to town on executorship business; he told me he was going to prove the will, and some other business, but I do not know whether it was for dividends; he did not always make entries of advances - I never made any against him; I do not recollect owing 18l. for hay - I owed the mother 15l.; on recollection, the 15l. 18s. 6d. is due to the estate; I have dealt with the mother for coals only; he paid attention to all orders given; the mother owes me 10l. - I owe the estate 15l.; I have tendered the balance; I made out an account, and gave it to Roake for his mother - the bill was not sent back, because the items for which the prisoner is indicted was not therein; Mr. Hyde had lived with him - he gave some information; the prisoner conducted himself well until within the last two months.
NOT GUILTY.
There were three other indictments against the prisoner for stealing malt and barley-meal, but no evidence was offered.
[trialAccount t18280221-233]
711. JOHN MILLINGTON was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of January, 50 lbs. weight of lead, value 8s., of Timothy Bramah and others.
MR. BODKIN conducted the prosecution.
GEORGE HOUNSON. I am gate-keeper at Messrs. Bramah's factory at Pimlico. Mr. Timothy Bramah is one of the firm. On the 12th of January, about five o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner was about to leave the premises - the plumbers leave at that hour - I searched all the men; I suspected something, and locked the gate. On searching the prisoner, I felt something hard in the band of his breeches - I said "There is something there," and desired him to take it out - he denied that he had any thing - it dropped from him, and proved to be lead; an officer was sent for, and he was taken into custody; the lead was weighed; it was the same description of lead as was on the premises.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. From where did it drop? A. From his trousers inside - I desired him to take it out. There are three Mr. Bramah's; he had been working there about eight days.
SAMUEL PEAKE. I am an officer, and took the prisoner; what the last witness has stated is true.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Did you compare the lead? A. No; it was sheet lead - he had knocked it up; Mr. Bramah makes no mark on his lead.
COURT. Q. It was not very convenient to have the lead dangling in his trousers for hours? A. No; he could not have done it.
DANIEL DAWKINS. I produce the lead - it weighs 52lbs.; I did not compare it - I received it from Howson.
Prisoner's Defence. The property is my own, and I took it there concealed, because I thought Mr. Bramah would claim it - I had no place to leave it at; and I brought it out, concealed in the same manner as I took it in.
GUILTY. Aged 29.
Confined Six Months and Whipped.
[trialAccount t18280221-234]
712. JAMES MORAN was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of January, 1 watch, value 1l. 16s., and 1 seal, value 4s., the goods of William Macoy.
WILLIAM MACOY. I live in Bloomsbury-square, and am servant to Mr. McIntosh. On the 16th of January, at night, about eleven o'clock, I was rather the worse for liquor, and had been at Mr. Maxwell's public-house, near Leather-lane - I do not know whether the prisoner was there - the watch was in my fob; I missed it while I was at Maxwell's - the prisoner might have been there without my knowing it; I looked at my watch at ten o'clock - whether I put into my fob, I cannot say; I might have dropped it; I went into the yard - I mentioned it in the house when I missed it; the next day I saw it at Hatton-garden - the prisoner was in custody; I never knew him.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You might have left the watch in the yard? A. Yes; it is more likely that I did than not. The prisoner said at the office that he had found it in the yard.
THOMAS MAXWELL. I am a licensed victualler - my house is in Holborn, above bars. The prisoner and the prosecutor were at my house; the prisoner is a working lad - he was at my house that night, with his father and other friends; I heard that the prosecutor had lost his watch, and I closed the doors immediately: the prisoner was gone - I had pushed him out of doors myself, that he might go home.
Cross-examined. Q. The boy heard no outcry about the watch? A. No. I have known him about seven years, and always found him honest.
ANDREW LLOYD I am an officer of Hatton-garden. I received information, and apprehended him; I asked where the watch was, and he said his mother had it; I was searching him, and he pulled it out of his right-hand pocket.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you hear that he had shown the watch to anybody? A. No; I received my information from the landlord's brother.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I went to the yard, and picked the watch up.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280221-235]
713. JOSEPH LITTLEWOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of February, 1 piece of tarpauling, containing 3 yards, value 10s., the goods of Joshua Beddall and Philip Beddall.
GEORGE STEPHENS. I am a labourer. Messrs. Beddalls live in High Holborn, and are carpenters. On the 13th of February, about seven o'clock in the morning, I was going to work at Mr. Turner's, about three doors from the building where the tarpauling was; that is in Middleton-street, Clerkenwell. I saw the prisoner coming out of the building, with it on his shoulder, and I stopped him; it was about three or four yards of old tarpauling - he said he was going home with it: he did not say where he lived, or that he worked for Beddall's.
WILLIAM TAYLOR. I work for Messrs. Beddalls - their names are Joseph and Philip; I worked for them in Middleton-street; I left the tarpauling nailed up the night before, when I left work at half-past six o'clock. The prisoner is a stranger.
JOSEPH COOMBES. I am a constable. The prisoner was delivered over to me - he said he had found the tarpauling in the building.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was going by the building, and went in for a necessary purpose; I saw the tarpauling laying, and I took it up; the place was all open.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Whipped and Discharged.
